## OF THE TERMS AND DOCTRINE

->OF ST.<-

# RAYMOND LULL

## DON ALONZO DE ZEPEDA

<summary>This title page introduces a polemical work by Alonzo de Zepeda, a Spanish military governor, defending the Trinitarian doctrines of Ramon Llull against a Jewish critic in Amsterdam. It reflects the intellectual climate of the 17th-century Spanish Netherlands, where military officers often engaged in sophisticated theological and philosophical debates.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Llull, Holy Trinity, Counter-Reformation, Amsterdam Synagogue, Spanish Netherlands, 17th-century theology, military authors, Franciscan Order</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page is the formal dedication of the work. Following the title page, the author, Don Alonzo de Zepeda, addresses his treatise not to a human patron—as was common for the era—but directly to the Trinity. This reflects the high spiritual stakes of the debate and the author’s background as both a soldier and a follower of Ramon Llull’s mystical philosophy.</meta>

# TO THE MOST HOLY
## AND INDIVISIBLE
# TRINITY.

->*Eternal and Most Powerful Lord.*<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'A' with ornate floral and scrollwork patterns.</image-desc>
Prostrate before the feet of your Divine Majesty, and with that veneration and fear by which the least of all creatures ought to approach your heavenly throne, I dedicate this discourse. And since it treats the defense of your incomprehensible *being* <note>original: "esse," a philosophical term for the core essence of God</note> and your adorable fruitfulness <note>original: "foecunditatis." In Llullian philosophy, this refers to the internal "activity" of God (the Father begetting the Son, etc.), which Zepeda is defending against critics who argued God must be static.</note>, its defense belongs to your Majesty: since no Prince of the world thrives with such power to undertake so arduous a task:

<meta>catchword: They are (original: Sunt)</meta>

<summary>This page contains the formal dedication of the treatise to the Holy Trinity. The author, writing with deep humility, argues that since the work concerns the very nature and internal fruitfulness of God, only God himself possesses the power to defend such a profound subject.</summary>
<keywords>dedication, Divine Majesty, Being of God, fecundity, Trinitarian theology, 17th-century devotion, Ramon Llull</keywords>

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication of the work to the Trinity. The author, Don Alonzo de Zepeda, addresses the Divine directly, positioning his theological treatise as a matter of divine defense rather than mere human scholarship. This reflects the 17th-century Spanish "baroque" style of piety, characterized by extreme humility ("the least of all your creatures") and high intellectual stakes.</meta>

# THE ADDRESS

...exists <note>original: "ra existit," likely the conclusion of a phrase like "vera existit" (truly exists) or "natura existit" (nature exists)</note>: and therefore all creatures must attribute to You in a more eminent way all the perfections which they admire in themselves, and consequently that internal fecundity <term>internal fecundity: in scholastic theology, this refers to the "fecundity of the nature," or God's ability to eternally produce the Son and the Holy Spirit within His own divine essence</term>, so that Your infinite essence and omnipotence may not suffer the defect of idleness <note>the author argues that a perfect, all-powerful God cannot be "idle" or inactive, but must be eternally active within Himself</note>.

->Therefore, Lord, who is like unto You?<-

You are God almighty, three in persons and one in substance: the Father unbegotten <note>original: "Pater ingenitus"; the Father is the source of the Trinity and is not generated by any other</note>, the Son only-begotten <note>original: "Filius unigenitus"</note>, and the Holy Spirit, who proceeds from both <note>This refers to the "Filioque" doctrine, the belief that the Spirit comes from both the Father and the Son together</note>, and eternally remains in both (constituting Your holy and undivided Trinity and one almighty God). His works are inseparable <note>original: "opera inseparabilia"; a core theological rule that because the Trinity is one God, all three Persons act together in every external work of creation or grace</note>, and His dominion shall endure forever. 

Let Judaism and Islam <note>original: "Mahometismus"</note> oppose [this] with the errors they have dreamed up, which Lucifer dictates to them for their own destruction. For in Your di- <meta>catchword: vi- [divinity]</meta>

<summary>This page concludes a logical argument for the Trinity, asserting that God's perfection requires an "internal fecundity" (the generation of the Son and Spirit) to avoid the defect of idleness. It defines the relationships within the Trinity and rejects the theological positions of Judaism and Islam as being inspired by Lucifer.</summary>

<keywords>internal fecundity, Father unbegotten, Son only-begotten, Holy Spirit, Filioque, inseparable works, religious polemics, Lucifer, divine essence</keywords>

# DEDICATION

...[admire] in themselves, and consequently that of internal fecundity <term>internal fecundity: translated from "fecundidad adintra," this refers to the theological concept that God is not "idle" but eternally active, generating the Son and breathing forth the Holy Spirit within His own essence</term>, so that Your infinite essence and omnipotence may not suffer the defect of idleness <note>The author argues that a perfect God must be eternally active within Himself to avoid the "flaw" of being unproductive</note>. 

->And so, Lord, who is like unto You?<-

You are almighty God, Triune in persons and one in substance: the Father unbegotten <note>original: "Padre ingenito"; the Father is considered the source of the Trinity, not created or generated by another</note>, the Son only-begotten <note>original: "Hijo unigenito"</note>, and the Holy Spirit, who proceeds from both and remains eternally within You, thus constituting Your holy and undivided Trinity, and one almighty God, whose works are inseparable <note>This refers to the doctrine that because the Trinity is one God, all three Persons act together in their works outside of themselves</note>, and whose dominion shall endure forever. 

Let Judaism and Islam <note>original: "Mahometismo," a common historical term for the Islamic faith</note> speak whatever errors they please, as dictated to them by Lucifer to plunge them into his depths. For in Your divine essence there are three coeternal and coequal Persons: Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.

<meta>The previous page concluded a spiritual dedication to the Holy Trinity. This page begins a new section: a formal "excusatory" or introductory letter addressed to a high-ranking secular patron, the Marquess of Caracena. The transition shifts from religious adoration to the social and political formalities of 17th-century authorship, where writers sought the protection and "patronage" of powerful nobles to validate their work.</meta>

# THE AUTHOR’S
## LETTER OF APOLOGY
### FOR HIS WORK.

->To the Most Excellent Lord, Lord Luis de Benavides, Carrillo and Toledo, etc., Marquess of Caracena, etc., Member of His Majesty’s Council of State, etc., Prefect of Artillery <note>original: "Rei Tormentariæ"; a technical term for the administration of cannons, engines of war, and ordnance.</note> throughout the whole Kingdom of Spain, as well as Generalissimo of the armies against the Portuguese <note>original: "Lusitanos"; the Latin term for the people of Portugal, referencing the contemporary conflicts between Spain and Portugal.</note> in the province today called Extremadura, etc.<-

I would incur the reputation of being the most ungrateful of men if, while attending to my obligations by publishing one of my labors, I were to implore any other human patronage than that of Your Excellency. But since this work is, by its very nature, consecrated to Him whose nature is divine, singu- <note>The text cuts off mid-word "singulare" (singular/unique) at the end of the page, as indicated by the catchword.</note>

# EXPLANATORY
## LETTER
### FROM THE AUTHOR.

To the Most Excellent Lord **Don Luis de Benavides**, **Carrillo y Toledo**, etc., <term>Marquis of Caracena</term><note>Luis de Benavides Carrillo (1608–1668) was a prominent Spanish nobleman and military commander who served as Governor of the Habsburg Netherlands and later led the Spanish forces against Portugal.</note>, etc., of His Majesty’s Council of State, Captain General of the Artillery of the Kingdoms of Spain, and of the army against Portugal on the Extremadura border, etc.

However, this work, as it dedicates itself by its own nature to the same one whose divine...

# LETTER
### XI

[...may God] preserve [your most Excellent] person for many most happy years (as is my heartfelt desire).
Brussels, November 30, 1666.

# PROLOGUE

Kind and Christian Reader. In this preface, I will not trouble you by being long-winded; for I shall keep it brief, promising that this treatise, though a Pygmy in its physical size <note>original: "Pigmæum in volumine." The author is using a common Baroque rhetorical device—the "modesty trope"—claiming his book is small in stature but great in importance.</note>, will be a Giant in its content (inasmuch as it is composed of the more sublime mysteries of our faith). In this period, "science" referred to any organized body of knowledge, including philosophy and theology.</note> have not closed their doors against arms, nor have they barred those who carry them from entry. # PROLOGUE
### TO THE
# READER.

Kind and Christian Reader. I will not trouble you by being long-winded in this Prologue: for I shall condense it into a few words, assuring you that this treatise, though a Pygmy in volume <note>original: "Pigmeo en volumen." A rhetorical "modesty trope" where the author minimizes the size of his book while magnifying its intellectual weight.</note>, is a Giant in what it contains, for it concerns one of the highest mysteries of our faith. And although discoursing upon such sublime things is foreign to a man of my profession <term>man of my profession: Zepeda was a career military officer; here he addresses the Baroque-era debate regarding "Arms vs. Letters," defending the right of a soldier to engage in high theological or philosophical scholarship.</term>; nevertheless, you should not judge it to be improper, for letters <note>original: "letras." This refers to the world of scholarship, literature, and the humanities.</note> have not closed their doors to arms <note>original: "armas." This refers to the military life and the profession of a soldier.</note>, nor have they disqualified them from advancement. While I am not unaware, that...

...[that their] scholars excelled in such great knowledge that they could understand even a single page of it) he forced me to make a promise, and upon his departure I kept that promise. ## TO THE READER.

...ry, and to the shame of our most holy religion, and could have redounded to the detriment of so great a mystery (by providing a greater incentive for their <term>Jewish obstinacy</term> <note>original: "Judaicam contumaciam." In 17th-century theological writing, this term was frequently used to describe the perceived stubbornness of those who refused to convert to Christianity.</note>); and also, spurred by my own conscience and by certain learned men who wished to see this controversy published, I have decided to commit it to the press in two languages (so that it may be circulated among those who are not fluent in Spanish).

Now, consider carefully whether honorable and urgent excuses for my confidence are lacking; and if, having them, I should wish to hide from the world that which the <term>Synagogue</term> <note>Referring here to the organized Jewish community and intellectual leadership in Amsterdam.</note> perhaps most unjustly turns to its own praise—by concealing my attack on their errors—in order to strengthen their own <term>perfidy</term> <note>original: "perfidiam." In the context of the time, this specifically meant "unbelief" or a state of being outside the "true faith."</note> and brand a greater disgrace upon our Most Holy Mother Church.

But if you weigh these causes without passion...

# TO THE READER.

*...stian)* you will be a defender of this work, seeking in it neither elegant phrases in one language nor the other, but only the substance. For besides the fact that the <term>subject matter is dogmatic</term> <note>original: "dogmatica." This indicates that the text deals with established religious doctrines and theological truths rather than being a literary or rhetorical exercise.</note>, I do not strive for such elegance in <term>Spanish</term> <note>original: "Española"</note>, and even less so in <term>Latin</term> <note>original: "Latina"</note>, in the practice of which <term>military occupations</term> <note>original: "ocupaciones militares." A significant personal detail suggesting the author spent much of his life as a soldier, using this to excuse his lack of academic polish.</note> have hindered me for many years.

And so I only advise you that, for the full understanding of some things in this discourse, it will be necessary for you to turn to the <term>Tree of Knowledge</term> <note>original: "Arbol de la Ciencia." A traditional Latin closing formula.</note>.

# CENSOR’S REVIEW
<note>original: "Censura." In the 17th century, this was a formal review by a church official to ensure a book contained no heresy before it was granted a license to be printed.</note>

**I**, Brother Peter Zorrilla Angulo—Theologian of the <term>Order of our Holy Father Saint Francis</term> <note>original: "Ordinis S. P. N. Francisci." The Franciscans often referred to Francis of Assisi as "Our Holy Father" (Sancti Patris Nostri).</note>, Guardian of the <term>Convent of Roermond</term> <note>A city in the Netherlands, then part of the Spanish Netherlands.</note>, former Commissary Visitor of the <term>Province of England</term> <note>A high-ranking administrative role overseeing Franciscan missions in England during a period of religious tension.</note>, and Preacher to the <term>Catholic King</term> <note>Referring to the King of Spain.</note> in both Royal Chapels of Castile and Burgundy—do hereby attest that I have found nothing in the aforementioned treatise: *Defense of the terms and doctrine of Ramon Llull, Enlightened Doctor, of the Order of Our Holy Father Saint Francis, regarding the mystery of the Most Holy Trinity, etc.*, written by **Lord Alfonso de Zepeda**, etc. (who likewise <term>rendered the work into Latin</term> <note>original: "latinitate donavit." This confirms Zepeda translated his own work from Spanish into Latin for a broader scholarly audience.</note>), which—adhering to the hidden but safe and entirely Catholic principles of Llull—is repugnant to the orthodox Faith or contrary to good morals.

Given at Louvain, in our <term>Convent of the Friars Minor</term> <note>The formal name for the Franciscan Order.</note>, on the 30th day of the month of October in the year 1666.

->**Brother Peter Zorrilla Angulo**, *the aforementioned, by his own hand.*<-

# CENSOR’S REVIEW
<note>original: "Censura." In the Spanish Netherlands during this period, a formal "Censura" was not necessarily a suppression of ideas, but a mandatory "peer review" by church authorities to certify that the book was safe for public consumption.</note>

Since the treatise on the terminology and doctrine of the <term>Illuminated Doctor</term> <note>original: "Illuminati Doctoris." The "Third Order" consists of laypeople who follow Franciscan spirituality while living in the world.</note>, concerning the *mystery of the Most Holy Trinity*—written against a certain *Respondent*, a *Jew in the <term>Amsterdam Synagogue</term>* <note>Amsterdam was a major center for the Jewish diaspora in the 17th century; Zepeda is framing his book as a response to a specific (though here unnamed) Jewish intellectual challenger.</note>, by the author Lord **ALFONSO DE ZEPEDA**, *Governor of the <term>Fort of Tolhuis</term> for the <term>Catholic King</term>* <note>The "Fort of Tolhuis" was a strategic military post on the Rhine; the "Catholic King" refers to the King of Spain, who ruled the Southern Netherlands at this time.</note>, etc.—has been duly and carefully read and examined, and since nothing has been found in the same treatise that is repugnant to the orthodox faith or good morals, it may be printed. 

Given at *Brussels* on the third day of November, in the year sixteen hundred and sixty-six.

->**MATTHEW MIDDEGAELS**<-

# L E T T E R S

## *Of Lorenzo Escudero, a Jew in the Amsterdam Synagogue, written to Lord Alfonso de Zepeda, Lieutenant of the General <term>Colonel</term>, etc.*
<note>original: "Chiliarchæ." A Greek-derived military term for a commander of a thousand men.</note>

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial letter 'C' featuring intricate floral and scrollwork patterns within a square frame.</image-desc>

Dear Sir, the book by **Ramon Llull** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315) was a Majorcan philosopher who developed a logical system (the "Art") intended to prove the truths of Christianity to non-believers through reason.</note> which Your Lordship gave to me as a gift, I have lent to a certain friend among our people who is considered by all to be quite learned. I did this so that he might examine it, to see if he understood it, and if it was as conclusive as Your Lordship had assured me. To this, he responds with what Your Lordship may see in the enclosed <term>pamphlet</term> <note>original: "quaternionem." A technical term for a small gathering of folded pages, often used for short reports or letters.</note>; I am sending it back to Your Lordship because it is curious. I do this not with the intention of striving to attack Your Lordship’s writings, but only so that you may admit that in our **Synagogue** <note>In the 17th century, the "Synagogue" often referred not just to the building, but to the entire organized Jewish community and its intellectual leadership.</note> there are also men well-versed in these matters. I remain always at Your Lordship's service; praying to the **Almighty** that He may guard your person, as I desire. Amsterdam, the first of June, 1663.

->*Your Lordship's servant, who kisses your hands,*<-
->L O R E N Z O  E S C U D E R O.<-
<note>Lorenzo Escudero (c. 1620–1683), also known by his Hebrew name Abraham Peregrino, was a former Spanish monk who converted to Judaism and became a prominent polemicist in Amsterdam.</note>

# LETTER

## Written by Lorenzo Escudero, a practicing Jew in the Amsterdam Synagogue, to the Lieutenant of the Field Master General, Don Alonso de Zepeda, etc.
<note>Lorenzo Escudero (died c. 1683) was a Spanish-born convert to Judaism who took the name Abraham Israel Peregrino. He was a prominent figure in the Sephardic community of Amsterdam.</note>

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut initial 'S' featuring scrollwork and floral motifs.</image-desc>

My Lord. *I lent the book by **Ramon Llull** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315) was a Majorcan philosopher who developed a logical system (the "Art") intended to prove the truths of Christianity to non-believers through reason.</note>, which Your Lordship gave to me, to a friend of those here whom we consider the most learned, to see if he understood it, and if it was as conclusive as Your Lordship had made me believe. Amsterdam, June 1, 1663.

->Your very humble servant, who kisses your hand<-
->LORENZO ESCUDERO.<-

# RESPONSE

## To a person who doubted whether the Book of Ramon Llull, newly translated and commented upon, was intelligible, and whether its arguments were conclusive.

<margin>A.</margin> Your Worship gave me a book that you brought from Brussels, so that I might examine it and tell you what I thought of its arguments. And speaking without the inexcusable weariness of introductions and preambles—and leaving aside those within the book itself as being immense and "head-breaking" <note>original: "frangitivos." The author is parodying Ramon Llull’s method of creating new technical words by adding suffixes like -tive and -able to Latin roots.</note> to any heads that might be "breakable" <note>original: "frangibles"</note>, for in those parts the "breaking-power" <note>original: "frangitivo"</note> is in excess, while the heads of readers lack the "capacity-to-endure" <note>original: "passientivo," a term derived from the capacity to undergo an action</note> such a listening to the many comings and goings of the **Illuminated Doctor** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315) was a Majorcan philosopher and mystic known by the title "Doctor Illuminatus."</note>, such that even the steadiest brain sinks into vertigo. Nevertheless, this was a means to "bonify" <note>To "bonify" (bonificar) is a specific term in Lullian logic meaning to make something good or to manifest its inherent goodness.</note> the book, so that even from this perspective, the act of "bonifying" and the "bonifiable" <note>original: "bonificable," referring to the potential of a thing to be made good or to receive goodness.</note> would not be absent.

# ATTACK ON THE DOCTRINE OF R. LULL

<margin>B.</margin> I respond, therefore, that I have shaken the leaves of this Lullian tree, and only the foliage has remained in my hands; and in the back of my head are so many "hæcceities" <note>original: "hæcceitates." From the Latin "haec," meaning "this." It is a philosophical term for "thisness," the quality that makes an object a specific, individual thing.</note> that I am unable to restore myself to my former "concretability" <note>original: "concrebilitatem." A parody of Lull's tendency to turn adjectives into abstract nouns ending in -ity.</note>; and therefore I proceed from "abstractability" to "abstractability" like some sort of universal Metaphysical entity; indeed, it is impossible to contract myself back to my innate singularity. Thus, **Platonic Ideas** possess greater tangibility; and they exist more solidly than I do myself "in reality" <note>original: "à parte rei." A technical Scholastic term meaning something exists objectively in the world, rather than just in the mind.</note>, from that moment when, on account of my sins, I walked through the garden, or the grove of the **Illuminated Doctor** <note>Ramon Llull.</note>. For he may well be called "Illuminated," yet the Tree has more "darkness-ity" <note>original: "tenebrositatis." The author mocks Lull by creating a clunky abstract noun for darkness.</note> (I would regret deriving this abstract term poorly) than "clarity" <note>original: "perspicuitatis."</note>. And since **Aristotle** (if I do not remember incorrectly) says in his books *On the Soul* <note>original: "Animæ." Referring to Aristotle's "De Anima," specifically Book II, Chapter 7.</note>, that *light is the act of the transparent* <note>original: "Lucem actum perfpicui esse." Aristotle argues that light is what makes a transparent medium (like air) actually transparent.</note>; since the Tree lacks transparency, it cannot be illuminated.

<margin>C.</margin> Nor can the alleged confusion communicate the rays of doctrine. Most poorly does an affected ambiguity distinguish the true from the false, using phrases and words which never <meta>catchword: were [fue-]</meta>

<margin>B.</margin>
I respond that I have leafed through the pages of this **Lullian Tree** <note>Referring to the *Arbor Scientiae* (Tree of Science), a famous work by Ramon Llull that uses a tree metaphor to organize all human knowledge.</note> with all due meditation, and I have been left with nothing but the leaves in my hands, and with so many "hæcceities" <note>original: "hecceidades." A term from Duns Scotus and scholastic philosophy meaning "this-ness," or the property that makes an individual thing unique.</note> in my skull that I cannot return to my own "concretability" <note>original: "concretibilidad." A mock-philosophical term invented by the author to parody Lull’s style of turning adjectives into abstract nouns.</note>. Thus, I wander about, turned into a metaphysical universal, drifting from "abstractability" to "abstractability," without it being possible to contract myself back to my native singularity. In short, **Platonic Ideas** have more tangibility and are more solid "in reality" <note>original: "à parte rei." A Latin technical term used in Scholasticism to describe something that exists objectively in the world rather than just in the mind.</note> than I am myself, ever since—for my sins—I walked through the Garden or Grove of the **Illuminated Doctor** <note>A traditional title for Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), a Majorcan philosopher and mystic.</note>. He may well be illuminated, but his Tree has more "tenebrosity" <note>original: "tenebroſidad." The author is punning here; he mocks Lull's style by creating a clunky abstract noun for "darkness."</note> (it would grieve me to derive this abstraction poorly) than clarity. And since **Aristotle** says—if I do not remember incorrectly—in his books *On the Soul* <note>original: "de anima." Referring to Aristotle's treatise on the nature of living things.</note> that *light is the act of the transparent* <note>original: "la luz es acto del perſpicuò." A classic Aristotelian definition: light is what makes a potentially transparent medium (like air) actually transparent.</note>, then the Tree, lacking transparency, cannot be illuminated.

<margin>C.</margin>
Nor can the intended confusion communicate rays of teaching; affected ambiguity, exquisite phrases, and never-before-used words are poor at discerning the true from the false.

<margin>D.</margin>

If for the former, it is an arrogant absurdity to wish to instruct them in the most basic elements of science, using accepted conclusions on debatable matters, and definitions or divisions that have already been established. But to place these sciences within "little trees" <note>original: *arboriunculis*. ...I abstract <note>original: *abstraigo*. This word continues from the end of the previous page.</note> without knowing why or from where). This is achieved without the harshness of technical terms, without excusable repetitions, and with a pleasant style that is proper and suited to the goal intended—namely, the intelligibility of the sciences on the part of the object. In this way, the sciences are made accessible, they become persuasive, and they are easily learned.

<margin>This is intelligible. <note>The author likely notes this to emphasize that his own proposed method of teaching is clear and easy to follow.</note></margin>

But to place these sciences within "little trees" <note>original: *arboriunculis*. A mocking diminutive referring to Llull’s *Arbor Scientiae*, a work that organized all human knowledge into complex tree diagrams.</note> filled with more "little houses" or chambers than the Temple of Solomon <note>A reference to the famously intricate and multi-roomed Temple described in the Bible; the author suggests Llull's system is unnecessarily labyrinthine.</note>, with such a swarm of words—which seem to be Castilian <note>Spanish</note> and are neither Latin nor Greek, even if everything seems like "Greek" <note>A common idiom meaning "incomprehensible" or "all Greek to me."</note> to the reader—and with so many dividing parts that, by comparison, the very **concept of being** <term>concept of being: *ratio entis*, the most fundamental and simple concept in Scholastic philosophy, representing the basic essence of anything that exists.</term> remains untouched and undivided: this is to seek to confuse rather than to teach.

<margin>**F.**</margin>

Furthermore, the fact that this "tree" is written in the Spanish language implies it is intended for those who do not know Latin, and who are consequently ignorant of most of the **liberal arts** <term>liberal arts: the core academic curriculum of the time, including grammar, logic, rhetoric, and the mathematical sciences.</term>; for if this were not so, who would excuse the poor taste of translating it? For—mercy me!—where can one find a man ignorant of Latin, **Logic**, **Physics**, and **Metaphysics**, who is capable of understanding even three lines of this book?

Indeed, a real distinction between units is required to form distinct numbers. Whether the essence is shared among all or is distinct, so long as the units themselves are really distinct, then where there are three really distinct Persons, a **triple number** <note>original: *ternarii numeri*</note> of Persons exists, not of essences, if by chance that essence is the same. 

Therefore, in the Trinity, discrete quantity must be admitted; for "Trinity" signifies the Persons rather than the Nature, in which unity and not plurality is given. Indeed, it is not permitted to say there are many essences; yet it is believed that there are three Persons, three relations, and three properties. ### Concerning the Mystery of the Most Holy Trinity

3. Returning to the reasoning of the *Tree*, I know not to whom it would not seem weak? Because <term>discrete quantity</term> <note>In scholastic philosophy, "discrete quantity" refers to things that are distinct and can be counted as individual units, such as numbers or distinct objects, as opposed to "continuous quantity" like space or a line.</note> consists of different units, from which groups of three, four, five, etc., are constituted. This requires a real distinction between the units in order to constitute different numbers; this remains true whether the essence were one in all of them or whether it were distinct, provided that the units themselves are really distinct. Therefore, wherever there might be three really distinct persons, there will be the **number of three** <note>original: "ternario"</note> of persons, not of essences (if the essence were the same). Consequently, in the Trinity, there will be discrete quantity, because "Trinity" signifies the persons more than the nature—in which there is unity and not plurality. For it is not confessed that there are many essences, yet it is believed that there are three persons, that there are three relations, and three properties. # Rebuttal of Ramon Llull’s Doctrine

...constitutes. For although the three <term>properties</term> <note>In Trinitarian theology, "properties" are the unique characteristics that distinguish the Persons: paternity (Fatherhood), filiation (Sonship), and procession (the Holy Spirit).</note> (as the theologians teach) are identified with the essence and are one and the same substance, they never **exist** <note>original: "existunt"</note> as the same property. Nor is it permitted to say that the property of **sonship** <note>original: "filiationis"</note> is that of **fatherhood** <note>original: "paternitatis"</note>; yet it is well stated that the essence of one is also the essence of the other—and this is by way of identity and not <term>formally</term> <note>A "formal" distinction implies a difference in the very definition or nature of the things, whereas an "identitative" distinction means they are the same thing viewed in different ways.</note>. Therefore, he was bound to say that all three persons or properties are one and the same divine essence, rather than saying they are one property. For a property is always considered as something that follows the essence. This is how it seems to me that Christian philosophers philosophize. It may well be that I am deceived, but I am not in error: for all the **Scholastics** <note>Scholasticism was the dominant philosophical and theological system of the medieval universities, emphasizing Aristotelian logic.</note> affirm that I speak most excellently.

5. Afterwards, he treats the unity of God and asserts that God is one (just as **Martin** <note>Martin is used here as a generic name for a person, similar to "John Doe," to illustrate a logical point about individuals.</note>, who is one in substance, and a horse, which is one). Truly, this is an unworthy comparison with the Majesty of the Divine essence, and even a false one. For Martin’s substance is numerically one and individual, and is composed <term>metaphysically</term> of **genus and difference** <note>In logic, a species is defined by its general category (genus, e.g., "animal") and its specific trait (difference, e.g., "rational").</note>, and <term>physically</term> of **matter and form** <note>The Aristotelian theory of hylomorphism, which states that all physical objects are composed of underlying matter and a specific shaping form.</note>

# Attack on the Doctrine of Ramon Llull

### Page 22
**Oppugnatio R. Lul. doctrinæ** <note>original: "Attack on the doctrine of Ramon Llull."</note>

exists. But God is **simply one** <note>This refers to the doctrine of "Divine Simplicity," which argues that God is not made of parts (like a body or a mind with different thoughts) but is a single, unified essence.</note> with the essential exclusion of all compositions that can be imagined; He does not fall under any **species** <note>In logic, a species is a sub-category; the author argues God cannot be categorized like a biological organism.</note>: He has neither individuality, nor higher predicates, nor lower ones.

In another way, he [Llull] also teaches that God is one in the same way that there is **one Sun, one Moon, etc.**, which is also completely false. For these, in comparison to the **divine unity** (which is not "one" in a numerical sense, but only **privatively** <note>original: "privativè"; meaning "one" in the sense that no other God exists, rather than "one" as the first number in a sequence.</note> insofar as no other God is possible) are only "one" **analogically**. We are not able to explain that infinite simplicity by any other term than "one." Therefore, those two modes which he applies to prove the unity of God are improper; they can only be admitted as a concession to our weak way of understanding.

In the following paragraph, regarding another point, he says: 
> Just like fire, which is not able to be dry through itself, but through the earth. 

This philosophy fights against all experience and the doctrine of both the ancients and the moderns. Because fire is hot and dry independently of the earth...

->**Concerning the mystery of the Most Holy Trinity** <note>original: "cerca SSS. Trinit. mysterium."</note><-

The author argues God is beyond all such categorization.</note> or species, nor does He have individuality, nor superior or inferior predicates.

In another way, he [Llull] says that He is one, like **one Sun or one Moon**. This is also false, because these are only **analogically** one <note>Meaning they are "one" in name only, but do not share the same kind of unity as God.</note> in comparison to the **divine unity**, which is not "one" in a numerical sense, but rather **privatively** <note>original: "privativamente"; meaning God is one in the sense that there is no other, rather than being "number one" in a series.</note> insofar as there is no other God. Nor can we explain that infinite simplicity of being by any other term than "one." Therefore, the two modes he tries to apply to prove the unity of God are improper, and can only be admitted as a concession to our weak way of understanding.

In the following paragraph, regarding another point, he says: 
> Just like fire, which is not able to be dry through itself, but through the earth. 

This philosophy is against all experience and the doctrine of both the ancients and the moderns, because fire, independent of the earth, is hot and dry; and each of

# Attack on the Doctrine of Ramon Llull

...plurality in God sounds wrong, even though it may be explained through the **Persons**; this is because, based on the **mode of signifying** <term>mode of signifying: the linguistic way a concept is expressed (Latin: *ex modo significandi*)</term>, it does not exclude the **Divine Essence** <note>The "Essence" refers to the single, undivided nature of God, which is distinct from the three "Persons" (Father, Son, and Holy Spirit). Therefore, the **Doctors** <note>The "Doctors" refers to the authoritative Teachers of the Church, such as St. Thomas Aquinas or St. Bonaventure.</note> use a different style: they do not ask *whether* there is plurality in God, but rather *in what way* plurality may be said to exist in God. And this "leaf" of his **Tree** <note>A reference to Ramon Llull’s *Arbor Scientiae* (Tree of Science), a massive encyclopedia where he organized all human knowledge into a tree-like structure. The author is metaphorically "pruning" Llull's errors.</note> should have been cut away, since it treats the subject of plurality in God so barrenly. Such a title might have been excellent for an era when many deities were worshipped, but not for this age, in which only one almighty God is known. This is a critique of his title, but it is not fitting to linger on titles. Let us proceed, therefore, to the substance of the matter.

He is these things fundamentally and by His very essence; any accidental form or name added to His essence would be incompatible with His nature.

# Attack on the doctrine of Ramon Llull

...ve, I understand it according to the distinction of a **formal cause** <term>formal cause: in traditional philosophy, the "form" or essence that gives a thing its specific identity, like the shape of a statue or the "humanity" of a person.</term>. Its effect is to give itself to the subject which it **informs** <term>inform: to provide the essential character or "form" to a subject.</term>, and to overcome its **potentiality** <term>potentiality: the capacity of matter to receive a form or to become something else.</term>.

**12.** However, if that which is considered as a form is not capable of being productive, it is futile to attribute to it the production of something like itself. This truth is clearly reflected in natural things. For although heat is the form of that which is hot, and is also active; it not only informs its own subject, but is also able to produce its likeness in another <note>original: "sibi simile in alio producere valet"; for example, a fire making a nearby object hot.</note>. But whiteness exists as the formal cause of a white thing; and being incapable of activity, it is not possible for it to generate another whiteness. And although the same logic found in creatures does not apply perfectly to the **Divine Majesty** <note>original: "divina Majestate"; a respectful term for God’s nature.</note>, we must still conceive of the "forms" we consider within Him: some as active, and others as perfecting but without activity (as we shall soon see).

**13.** Returning again to our main argument. The eternity of God is God Himself: and if, by a **fiction of reason** <note>original: "rationis fictione"; a term for a mental concept or abstraction used to understand something that is actually a single, simple reality.</note>, it is considered as a form, the eternity of God would be "eternizing" <note>original: "æternificativa"</note>, that is, the reason on account of

...ee, in contrast to the **formal cause** <term>formal cause: the essential nature or "recipe" of a thing that makes it what it is.</term>, whose effect is to give itself to the subject that it **informs** <term>inform: in a philosophical sense, to give "form" or essence to matter.</term>, and to overcome its **potentiality** <term>potentiality: the state of being able to become something, which is "completed" once it receives a form.</term>.

**12.** But if that which is considered as a form is not capable of being productive, it is in vain to attribute to it the production of its like. In natural things, this truth is seen with evidence. Because although heat is the form of that which is hot, it is also active; and thus, besides informing its subject, it can produce its likeness in another. However, whiteness is the formal cause of that which is white, and being incapable of activity, it is not possible for it to produce another whiteness. And although the same reasoning found in creatures does not apply to the **Divine Majesty** <note>original: "Magestad divina"; referring to the nature of God.</note>, it is necessary that of the forms we consider in Him, some are conceived as active, and others as perfecting but without activity, as we shall soon see.

**13.** Returning to the point. The eternity of God is God Himself; and if it is considered through a **conceptual fiction** <note>original: "ficcion de razon"; a logical tool where theologians treat different aspects of God’s single essence as if they were separate "parts" to help human understanding.</note> as a form, it would be the "eternizing" eternity of God, **that is** <note>original: "id est"</note>, that by which God is eter-

...that God exists as eternal, and the same is true regarding His goodness, greatness, and unity. This is because all those things are nothing other than God Himself, who by His own essence is indivisibly eternal, good, and one.

# Attack on the Doctrine of Ramon Llull

...of reasoning; and eternity does not **eternify** <note>original: "æternificat"; a term coined by Ramon Llull to describe eternity acting as an active force.</note> in an active sense; neither one attribute nor the other signifies an active thing, but rather a **perfective** <term>perfective: relating to the state of being perfect or complete; here, it means these attributes describe what God *is* rather than acting as separate tools He uses.</term> one. Nor does it follow—as is often the intent of the **Tree** <term>the Tree: a reference to Ramon Llull's *Arbor Scientiae* (Tree of Science), a massive encyclopedic work where he organized all branches of knowledge into metaphorical trees with roots, branches, and fruits.</term>, though in which specific branches or clusters of leaves I do not know—that the almighty unity would fail to be **possificative** <note>original: "possificativa"; from "posse" (to be able), meaning the power to make things possible.</note> and **magnificative** <note>original: "magnificativa"; the power to make things great.</note>, nor that such an eternity would fail to coexist, simply because all these attributes did not have the equal power to "make-good" or "unify" something else. 

This is a kind of **sophistry** <term>sophistry: a clever but false or circular argument.</term> and lacks any basis in reality. For if we look into the truth of the matter, the Lord God exists as all His attributes in the most absolute **simplicity** <term>simplicity: in theology, the doctrine that God is not "made of parts" but is one undivided essence.</term>. Therefore, none of these attributes is hindered in its operation; although we do not attribute to each individual quality what belongs to the whole, nor does one attribute produce the specific effects of another. This is because when His Divine Majesty produces anything, that product comes from a single, truly indivisible source.

### Attack on the Doctrine of Ramon Llull

We do not grasp how anything exists within the infinite and incomprehensible **Creator**. Although theological meticulousness claims the opposite for itself by proposing a specific question—namely, in what the "essence of God" formally consists—it remains ignorant of its own nature, and even that of a lowly animal. These are **Icarian intellects** <note>A reference to Icarus from Greek mythology, who flew too close to the sun on wings of wax and fell. The author uses this to describe thinkers whose intellectual pride leads to their downfall.</note>, who cast themselves down while they strive to climb higher; they do not perceive that they are being driven headlong until they find themselves overwhelmed in the deep sea of human ignorance. 

How God understands, how He knows, how He wills and loves, no one penetrates, nor is our material nature able to attain such knowledge. Therefore, there is no foundation for the "tree" to foolishly produce flowers <note>Ramon Llull often used the metaphor of a "Tree of Science" where "flowers" represented the preliminary logical principles used to reach "fruits" or conclusions. The author here calls these deductions "foolish."</note>—namely, the argument that if **Unity** does not unify something else, it will possess "minority," <gloss>being lesser</gloss> because it would be lesser than the other attributes.

The author is critiquing Llull’s habit of using mechanical or craft-based analogies to explain how God "works."</term> who fashions a nail. For it is a grave error to compare the imperfect ways that created things operate with the inaccessible ways of the Creator.

17. Nor can it be said that this is merely according to our human way of understanding;

For if he speaks in respect to God Himself, it is the same as saying that unity exists in a **primary act** <term>primary act: in scholastic philosophy, *actus primus* refers to the initial state of being or having a capacity, as opposed to its exercise.</term> toward working as one; and that God is **in potency** <term>in potency: the capacity to become or do something that is not yet realized.</term> so that He might become such through a single formal act, which exists as the exercise of unity, called "to unify." 

But if this is understood in respect to another <note>Meaning something outside of God's own essence.</note>, this would be to say that the unity of God, through a single act of unifying, had unified—or created—another person who was potentially "unifiable." But all of this is merely talk and an affectation of words that seem to suggest something about God; in reality, they signify nothing but the defects of our own fabricated ways of understanding. For if the author had said that the "unifying power," the "unifiable," and the "act of unifying" were **really distinct** <note>In theology, a "real distinction" implies that things are actually different entities, whereas a "formal" or "rational" distinction exists only in the mind. #### Concerning the mystery of the Most Holy Trinity.

Insofar as they are God Himself, what is **truly** <note>original: "realmente." In Scholastic philosophy, this implies "in reality" as opposed to "conceptually."</note> affirmed of one is affirmed of all, because everything is identically one; but as they are conceived by the **fashioning intellect** <term>fashioning intellect: the human mind's ability to create conceptual categories or "fictions" to help understand a reality that is actually a single, undivided whole.</term> as diverse, none **formally** possesses what the other has: neither does the intellect will, nor does the will understand. 

And if this is not the case, let the **Tree** <note>Reference to Ramon Llull’s *Arbor Scientiae* (Tree of Science).</note> say why the second Person of the Trinity is not the Spirit, and why the Spirit is not the Son, since both are produced by the origin of a living thing from a living thing, in the likeness of nature through a **joined principle**? 

For in truth, he will not find the answer in any **leaflet** <note>original: "hojita." The author is making a pun: a "leaflet" is both a small leaf on Llull's metaphorical tree and a page in his book.</note> of the Tree, since the **Ancient Doctors** did not find it in many leaves [pages], and they resolved that there was no intellect that could grasp it, nor provide the reason for the difference, and that it was impossible to penetrate it. 

Thus it is stated by: 
* **Athanasius**, *Epistle to Serapion*; Sermon 3 *Against the Arians*. 
* **Basil**, [Book] 15 *On the Trinity*, Chapter 19. 
* **Augustine**, *Tractate 99 on John*. 
* **Ambrose**, Book 1 of *On the Faith*, Chapter 9. 
* **Augustine**, Book 3 *Against Maximinus*, Chapter 14. 
* **John Damascene**, Book 1 of *On the Orthodox Faith*, Chapter 16. 

And among the Scholastics: 
* The **Master of the Sentences** <note>Peter Lombard (c. 1096–1160), whose *Sentences* was the standard theological textbook of the Middle Ages.</note> in Book 5, Distinction 13. 
* **Gabriel** <note>Gabriel Biel (c. 1420–1495), a late Scholastic philosopher.</note>. 
* **Gregory** <note>Likely Gregory of Rimini (c. 1300–1358).</note>.

### Attack on the Doctrine of Ramon Llull

...[it] is also an agent. But quantity, in relation to a quantifiable substance, through its own formal effect (if he <note>Referring to Ramon Llull.</note> had chosen to call this "quantifying"), will be "quantificative" <note>This is a technical term describing the quality of giving size or "quantity" to a thing.</note>. And in the case of "whiteness," in relation to its presence in a subject, it is also fitting to grant a "whitening" agent (which is the whiteness itself), a "whitenable" thing (which is the subject), and the act of "whitening," which becomes the formal act.

However, quantity will not "quantify" anything external besides its own subject; nor will whiteness "whiten" anything other than that in which it resides. This is because these are "forms" or "rationales of naming" <note>original: "rationes denominandi." In scholastic philosophy, these are attributes that describe what a thing *is* (e.g., "God is good") rather than what a thing *does* (e.g., "God creates").</note>, not rationales of production. In this way, eternity will "eternify" that which is eternal; unity will "unify" its own one; and goodness will "bonify" <note>original: "bonificabit." To make or designate as good.</note> its own good (which is God).

All of this is according to our human way of understanding, as has been said. But none of these rationales will unify *another* being, nor will they "bonify" another (acting as a producer of it, or as the reason for its production). For when we consider these as though they were "forms" in God, or similar to our conceptual understanding of forms, we do not view them as active principles <note>original: "rationes activas."</note>, but rather as "perfective" ones <note>Principles that describe the inherent perfection and completeness of God's essence rather than His outward actions.</note>. For when we consider these in God as **forms**, or concepts in the manner of forms, we do not consider them as **active rationales**, but rather as **perfective** ones <note>Attributes that signify a state of completion or perfection within God Himself, rather than forces that must create something outside of Him.</note>, and...

<meta>This page continues the refutation of Ramon Llull’s doctrine of "Dignities." # Refutation of Ramon Llull’s Doctrine

...it becomes most proper, since in God such forms—on account of which He is one, good, and eternal—are not actually distinct. Therefore, that logical consequence <note>The author is referring to a specific argument made by Ramon Llull.</note> turns out to be fallacious: *“Therefore, if unity is not productive, it will be lesser than omnipotence.”* 

This is because in **omnipotence** <term>omnipotence: God’s all-encompassing power to act and create</term>, which is the **essence** <term>essentia: the fundamental nature or "being" of God</term> itself, the act of “producing” is real. However, the consideration of these forms as if they were distinct from omnipotence and essence is a fiction; it exists only in our reasoning and depends solely on our own way of understanding. 

It is nothing more than saying that our intellect, insofar as it considers God as a thinking being, does not [at that moment] consider Him as the **Creator**. For in effect, “to understand” and “to create” do not exist as one and the same thing in our concepts; just as when He loves, He [in that conceptual moment] does not punish or reward. 

Thus, in the same way, insofar as He is “one,” He is not considered “eternal”; and insofar as He is “good,” He is not considered “one.” This is not because all these attributes do not coexist in reality as one and the same thing, but because the human intellect invents a diversity where only pure identity dwells. From this, it clearly appears that although God is the producer of His creatures, the **formality** <term>formality: the specific logical definition or "category" of a concept</term> of “oneness” is not the rationale for producing them.

### Concerning the Mystery of the Most Holy Trinity

Since this is improper—because in God there are no such **forms** by which He is one, good, or eternal—this logical consequence is fallacious: *“Therefore, if unity is not productive, it is lesser than omnipotence.”* This is because in **omnipotence** <term>omnipotence: God's all-encompassing power</term>, which is the very **essence** <term>essence: the fundamental nature or "being" of God</term> itself, the act of "producing" is real. However, the consideration of these forms as being distinct from omnipotence and from the essence is a fiction of reason <note>original: "fingida, y de razon"</note>, depending solely on our own understanding.

It is nothing more than saying that our intellect, insofar as it considers God as an intelligent being, does not consider Him as the **Creator**. This is because, in their effects, "to understand" and "to create" are not one and the same. Likewise, insofar as He wills <note>Spanish: "quiere." In this context, it refers to the Divine Will in its aspect of love or mercy.</note>, He does not punish; and insofar as He punishes, He does not [conceptually] will or reward.

This is not because all these attributes are not really a single unity in Him, but because the intellect invents a diversity where there is only pure identity. Through this, it is clearly seen that although God is the producer of His creatures, the reason for producing them is not the **formality** <term>formality: a scholastic term for a specific aspect of a thing that the mind treats as distinct, even if it isn't separate in reality</term> of being "one," even though in reality all de-

# Opposition to the Doctrine of Ramon Llull

A more difficult argument exists, which the *Tree* <note>A reference to Ramon Llull’s *Arbor Scientiae* (The Tree of Science), a work where he uses a "tree" metaphor to organize all human knowledge according to his logical system.</note> intends to prove: that because God is "one," He must produce another "one"—which he calls the **unifier** <term>unifier: in Llull’s system, the active principle that performs the action of making something one</term>—and another which is **unifiable** <term>unifiable: the passive principle that receives the action of being made one</term>. And in truth, if the essential nature of the "one" is scrutinized, the argument involves a poorly constructed **sophism** <term>sophism: an argument that seems clever but is actually based on a logical fallacy</term>. It is framed by him in this way: "God is one; therefore, by this logic of oneness, He is bound to produce another 'one' equal to Himself, which is 'one' just as He is, and is not something else, so that the power to **unify** <note>original: *unificare*</note> is not lacking in Him."

To me, the contradiction in the conclusion seems as inherent as the truth of the premise. Indeed, an argument formed in the opposite way concludes as follows: "God is essentially one; therefore, He cannot produce another 'one' like Himself." # Opposition to the Doctrine of Ramon Llull

...essentially exists as one; and another "one" cannot be produced without it being distinct. Therefore, the producer would no longer be one, because both the "one" and the "other" would exist together. It is as if the essence of Adam consisted in being a single "one"; in that case, it would be a contradiction for any other human being to exist besides Adam.

There is no reason to mock this line of reasoning, for I am not ignorant of the distinction used to answer it. I make the following distinction: Is it "another" in essence? I deny this. Is it "another" in personality? I grant it. But if personality and essence are actually the same thing in reality, then that "one" which produces "one" must necessarily produce "another" distinct thing.

Nor are the subtleties of the **Scholastics** <term>Scholastics: Medieval theologians and philosophers who used a highly structured logic to reconcile Christian faith with Aristotelian reason.</term> hidden from me—those they use to evade this difficulty and adapt their principles. For instance, the principle: *those things which are the same as a third thing are the same as each other* <note>original: *quæ sunt eadem uni tertio*</note>. Nor am I unaware of that other distinction: *unless an opposition of relation stands in the way* <note>original: *si non obstiterit relationis oppositio* — a standard theological rule stating that in the Trinity, the Persons are identical to the divine essence except where they are defined by their relationship to each other, such as Father and Son.</note>. I am also familiar with the **Thomistic** <note>Referring to the followers of St. Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274).</note> distinction of **reasoned reason** <term>reasoned reason: *ratio ratiocinata*, a distinction created by the mind but based on a real foundation in the object itself.</term> and **formal** distinction, as well as the distinction of **Scotus** <note>Duns Scotus (c. 1266–1308), known as the "Subtle Doctor," who proposed a "formal distinction" that exists in reality between the essence and the personhood of God.</note> which is said to exist **from the nature of the thing** <term>from the nature of the thing: *ex natura rei*, a distinction that is real and independent of any mental operation.</term>.

However, if all these arguments are weighed carefully, the point always remains in its original difficulty and unintelligibility. It remains, as it was before, insoluble; it is believable only as a **dogma of faith** <term>dogma of faith: A truth revealed by God that must be believed, even if it cannot be fully understood or proven by human logic.</term>, not as something that can be understood through discourse or logical persuasion.

# Regarding the mystery of the Most Holy Trinity

...*another* produced without being *another*, so that the producer would no longer be **one**, because there would be **one and another**. For example, if the essence of Adam were to be **one**, it would be a contradiction for there to be **another** man besides Adam.

There is no reason to laugh at this little argument, for I am not ignorant of the distinction: is it **another**? I **distinguish**; in essence, I **deny** it; in personality, I **concede** it. However, if personality and essence are actually one and the same thing, then the **one** who produces **one** must necessarily produce **the other**.

Nor are the clever subtleties of the **Scholastics** <term>Scholastics: Medieval theologians and philosophers who used highly structured logic to reconcile Christian faith with reason.</term> hidden from me—those they use to evade this difficulty and reconcile the principle: *those things which are the same as a third thing [are the same as each other]* <note>original: *quæ sunt eadem uni tertio, &c.* — a fundamental rule of logic: if A=C and B=C, then A=B. Here, it is applied to the Persons of the Trinity and the Divine Essence.</note> and that other rule: *unless an opposition of relation stands in the way* <note>original: *si non obstiterit relationis oppositio* — a standard rule stating that the Persons of the Trinity are identical to the divine essence except where they are defined by their relationship to each other, such as Father and Son.</note>. I am familiar with the **Thomistic** <note>Referring to the followers of St. Thomas Aquinas.</note> distinction of *reasoned reason* <note>original: *rationis ratiocinatæ* — a distinction that exists in our minds but is based on a reality within the object itself.</note>, and the *formal distinction from the nature of the thing* <note>original: *formal, ex natura rei*</note> used by **Scotus** <term>Scotus: John Duns Scotus (c. 1266–1308), an influential Franciscan philosopher and theologian known for his complex "formal distinction."</term>.

But even if one carefully notes the arguments of both sides, the point remains in its difficulty and unintelligibility, as insoluble as before. It is only believable as a dogma of faith; it is not something that can be reasoned, nor persuaded, nor known.

For to strive for the generation of gold from a **sublimation vessel** <note>original Latin: *sublimatorio* — a piece of laboratory equipment used in alchemy to purify substances by heating them into vapor and collecting the deposit. The author compares Llull's logical "Art" to the futile pursuits of alchemy.</note>, and for a general doctrine for knowing everything from the **Tree** <note>A reference to Llull’s *Arbor Scientiae* (Tree of Science), where he organized all human knowledge into encyclopedic branches.</note>; this is to **seek pears from an elm tree** <note>original Latin: *pyra ab ulmo petere* — a classical proverb meaning to expect something from a source that cannot produce it, or to attempt the impossible.</note>. And the doctrine of Llull has demanded judgment from many, but it has returned it to them <note>The author implies that rather than granting wisdom, the system leaves its students bewildered or stripped of their common sense.</note>. And in summary, only the Tree of Paradise taught the knowledge of good and evil, but this one of Ramon Llull teaches neither good nor evil. May God protect **Your Lordship** <note>Latin: *D. V.* (Dominationem Vestram) — a formal honorific address used for high-ranking officials or nobility.</note>, and free you from "watered" authors—who neither grow green nor bear fruit unless by the continuous irrigation of commentary—and may He supply what is lacking. For I have played the part of a **Theologian**, when I am but a mere **Layman**, bearing sword and cloak—that is, I am a secular man.

->THE END.<-

...general doctrine for knowing everything, this is to **ask for pears from an elm tree** <note>A traditional idiom meaning to attempt the impossible or to look for something from a source that cannot provide it.</note>; and the doctrine of **Llull** <note>referring to Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316), the Majorcan philosopher and mystic who created a complex logical system intended to prove the truths of Christianity.</note> has demanded the power of judgment from many, but only returned it to them in confusion. Hastenining, therefore, to the response of all the Chapters. To point 1. A.B. I say: I am greatly astonished, and with the greatest reason, that the author (whom I shall hereafter call the **Respondent**) striving to be esteemed a physicist and a Metaphysician, holds **abstract terms** in such low regard. For indeed he ought not to be ignorant of the fact that the more our intellect achieves greater abstractions in any being, the closer it will approach the knowledge of its essence; and the more precisely it exercises the **analytic art** <term>analytic art: the method of breaking down complex concepts into their simplest, most fundamental components</term>, the greater the understanding it will acquire of their properties and essences. For this reason, that science is judged more perfect which demonstrates truer and more particular clarifications of its subject. And since this is the case, why does the Respondent so ignominiously reject the abstract terms of **Ramon Llull**? For it is through these very terms that our intellect reaches the ultimate knowledge of a thing...

# Defense of the doctrine of Ramon Llull

<note>original: "Reubau." This is a technical acronym in Lullian philosophy representing the five transcendental properties of being: *Res* (thing), *Unum* (one), *Bonum* (good), *Aliquid* (something distinct), and *Verum* (true).</note>
That is, that it is a thing, one, good, true,
and distinct from another (this explanation 
is only for your sake, **Lord Lawrence**, 
not for the **Lord Respondent**, because I 
least doubt that all these things are most 
familiar to him; but not the abstractions of

of the being itself, and those things from 
which this composite stands; so that we 
can attain the knowledge we desire 
concerning being. Namely, that "being" 
presupposes "to be" <note>original: "esse," referring to the act of existence.</note>, and that existence 
presupposes essence (for "to be" exists 
on account of the principle of essence; 
and not essence on account of the 
principle of "to be"); that essence 
has a nature, for otherwise it would be 
fictitious; that this nature possesses 
quiddity, or it would be nothing, and 
consequently all things which we see 
and touch as real beings would not exist. 
Therefore, the **Respondent** rejects the 
abstractions of **Ramon Llull** most 
improperly; since by means of them 
we reach the knowledge of a most 
well-founded and higher **Metaphysics**, 
and a truer **Philosophy**; they by no 
means produce the darkness in which 
the **Respondent** [fumbles]...

->Defense of the doctrine of Ramon Llull<-
<meta>catchword: tan-</meta>

...is deceived; but on the contrary, it produces the most brilliant radiance for anyone who applies himself to the attentive reading of his **illuminating trees** <note>original: "arborum"; a reference to Ramon Llull’s *Arbor Scientiae* (Tree of Science), a systematic work where he uses the metaphor of a tree to organize all human knowledge into roots, trunks, and branches.</note> and contemplates the sublime and supreme method that he maintains in the <term>enucleation</term> <note>From the Latin "enucleatio," meaning to remove the kernel from the husk; hence, a clear and thorough explanation or unfolding of a complex subject.</note> of all sciences and of his own most profound doctrine. From this, it is most clearly evident that he was inspired by God, as so many and such authentic authors affirm—the ones I have cited in the history of his life. The reliability of these authors is to be held as secure and firm as that of all other historians in the whole world; otherwise, it would be necessary to reject them all as tellers of fables and to judge their histories (whether secular or sacred) as invented and **chimerical** <note>Referring to the "Chimera," a mythical hybrid creature; here meaning "illusory" or "fanciful."</note>. I expect that the Respondent will by no means approve of such a conclusion, nor will he any longer call into doubt the divine illumination of the Blessed **R. Llull**.

Nor are there signs of a mature judgment in the Respondent's desire to attribute to a defect of science, or of the **Master** <note>Referring to Ramon Llull as the teacher of this system.</note>, the fact that he is not easily understood by everyone. For by that same reasoning, **Hermes**, **Socrates**, **Plato**, and **Aristotle**, the **Princes of Philosophy**...

# Defense of the doctrine of R. Llull

And it is falsely supposed and imagined that the style of **Ramon Llull** in theological matters is contrary to the assertions of the **Angelic Doctor** <note>Saint Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the preeminent Scholastic theologian whose work Llull is here being compared to.</note>: for he follows him, by naturally deriving terms and words that are more representative of things; as is seen most clearly in his "hundred forms," in his abstracts, and in the decrees of his principles; and in other terms which cannot be expressed except through newly-invented words, which ought to be embraced as dogmatic. Which very many theologians have also used, and use daily in the schools. # Defense of the Doctrine of Ramon Llull
<note>original Latin: *Propugnatio R. Lul. doctrinæ*</note>

...[materia]ls, which end only in the word <note>original: *voce*, referring to the spoken name or label</note>; while in their meaning flows such great marrow and substance.

<margin>D.</margin> Regarding point D. Most certainly, I do not understand what is related in this paragraph; since it is established among all people, beyond any form of doubt, that every Author preparing a scientific <term>scientific: *scientificum*, referring here to "scientia" or organized knowledge/demonstrative logic</term> book endeavors to compose it as something universal, both for beginners <note>original: *tyronibus*, a term for recruits or students</note> in that science, as well as for those who are advanced and mature in it. He does this so that the former may be taught and learn, and the latter may contemplate, and by further clarifying <note>original: *enucleando*, literally "shelling out" or extracting the kernel of an idea</note> the matter of the object and their subject at the same time, they may discover greater insights. And for this reason, it is necessary to begin from the first elements for beginners, and for the learned to derive conclusions already accepted or debatable, and even definitions, divisions, and appropriate subdivisions: so that the essences and natures of things (about which there is controversy) may be made clear. This is the path that **Ramon Llull** pursues in the *Elemental Tree* <note>original: *Arbore elementali*, one of Llull's works where he applies his "Art" to the physical world and the four elements</note> and his other works. Otherwise, all sciences would exist as chaos itself; and in them, neither a beginning nor an end would be attained. It is therefore fitting...

It emphasizes that Llull's structured method in works like the "Elemental Tree" prevents scientific knowledge from devolving into chaos.</summary>

He does this so that the former may learn, and the latter may contemplate; by "gutting" the subject matter <note>original: *desentrañando*, a vivid term meaning to extract the core or "entrails" of a topic</note>, they may discover greater knowledge of the object and, at the same time, of its subject.

it is a work for obtaining all sciences; and he reduces each individual science to seven chapters or classes, which consist in any tree; thus sparing us from the immensity of precepts, rules, terms, and propositions that commonly roam through the schools <note>A reference to the "Scholastic" method of education dominant in universities, which Llull sought to simplify</note>. From this, it is very easily gathered that the **Respondent** <note>original: *Rẽs.*, an abbreviation for *Respondens*. # Defense of the Doctrine of Ramon Llull

...asserting that the best Latin is nothing but mere Castilian <note>original: *Castellanis*. Castilian refers to the Spanish language</note> phrases. However, Lord Lawrence, if the Respondent himself does not grasp the **Tree** <note>original: *Arborem*. This refers to Llull's *Arbor Scientiae* (The Tree of Science), a massive encyclopedia that organized all knowledge into a single logical tree-structure</note>, why should the fault be ascribed to me? It is even less my fault that the Respondent, despite having studied in his time, follows the common opinion of the dull-witted—those who think, forsooth, that the sciences can neither be taught nor explained except in the Latin language. 

But I ask him: were the sciences originally constructed in such a language? Because if that were the case, his argument would rest upon some foundation, however superficial. Yet it is obvious even to the "bleary-eyed and the barbers" <note>original: *lippis & tonsoribus*. A Latin idiom (from Horace) meaning "it is common knowledge" or "everyone knows it"</note> that the **Egyptians** drew their knowledge from the **Chaldeans**, the **Greeks** from the Egyptians, and the **Latins** from the Greeks—each one translating those sciences into their own **vernacular idiom** <term>vernacular idiom: the language naturally spoken by the people of a specific country or region, as opposed to a learned scholarly language like Latin</term>. This same practice is quite commonplace today among all European nations; therefore, I have no doubt that we **Spaniards** ought to enjoy the same privilege.

### Defense of the Doctrine of Ramon Llull

...my **illuminated Master** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316) was known as the *Doctor Illuminatus* because he claimed his system of logic was revealed to him by God on Mount Randa</note> has dispersed in various places.

This doctrine of Ramon Llull regarding **concretes** and **intrinsic correlates** <note>In Llull's "Great Art," every attribute like Goodness or Power has three internal components: an agent (e.g., the "Bonificative"), a recipient (the "Bonificable"), and the act itself (the "Bonifying"). These are the "correlates"</note> is the same as that which **Aristotle** taught (though understood by only a few). For in Book 1 of *On the Heavens and the World* <note>original: *de coelo & mundo*</note>, along with many other philosophers, he pronounces: *Whatever being is perfect has the power of acting and of being acted upon.*

And this is manifest; otherwise, it would possess neither a nature nor a **quiddity** <note>from the Latin *quidditas*, meaning the "what-ness" or the very essence of a thing</note>. Therefore, every real essence has the power of operating and of being acted upon, or else it will not be a being, nor a perfect essence in the category of being. For in that case, **Reubau** <note>A scholastic mnemonic/acronym for the "transcendental" properties of being: *Res* (Thing), *Ens* (Being), *Unum* (One), *Bonum* (Good), *Aliquid* (Something), *Verum* (True). The author uses this to show that if a thing exists, it must possess these fundamental qualities</note> would not apply to it, and thus it would not be a **thing that is one, good, true**, and distinct from any other. 

Therefore, if it is a being and contains **Reubau** within itself, it is necessary that it possess the power of acting and of being acted upon; otherwise, it would not be endowed with a "nature," which exists as the principle of all motion. And if it possessed that nature but the power remained unused, it would be idle and devoid of a **purpose** <note>original: *finis*; referring to the "final cause" or goal toward which every natural thing strives</note>. Therefore, to avoid all these logical inconsistencies, the aforementioned power must not be denied to any real essence. And this is true not only in relation to another thing, but even with respect to the essence itself:

<summary>This page argues that Ramon Llull’s theory of "correlatives" (internal active and passive principles) is supported by Aristotle’s physics. It asserts that for any being to truly exist and possess a nature, it must have the power to act and be acted upon internally.</summary>

Therefore, to avoid all these inconveniences, one must concede this aforementioned power to every real essence. ### Defense of R. Lull’s *doctrine*

For no being can characterize another unless it is itself of the same nature as the characterization it provides to the other. As **Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher (384–322 BC), whose works formed the foundation of medieval scholasticism</note> indicates in various places, this is held as a well-worn axiom in Philosophy.

The same Philosopher corroborates our aim in the place cited, asserting: ***There are three things in any given thing, namely: nature, power, and operation.*** <note>original Latin: "Tria sunt in qualibet re, videlicet natura, virtus & operatio"</note> Therefore, even in any real essence—which is a "thing"—these three must be found. However, **power** <term>virtus: in this context, the inherent capacity or potential for a thing to act or be acted upon</term> is either active or passive; thus, every essence flourishes through these powers, and also through the **nature** from which they emanate.

It follows, then, that the nature of no thing can be granted without this intrinsic power of acting and being acted upon; otherwise, it would fall into a void of purpose produced by its own **inactivity** <note>original Latin: "otiositate," implying a state of being "idle" or useless because it lacks the capacity to fulfill its function</note>. Therefore, to reject such an absurdity, it is necessary to concede that in every essence, nature, power, and operation exist not only externally, but also intrinsically and immediately. Otherwise, things would be rendered empty within themselves and without a goal, if they did not possess those three components which Aristotle taught exist in every thing or being.

<summary>This page uses Aristotelian axioms to argue that every real essence must possess an internal nature, power, and operation. Without these intrinsic active and passive capacities, a thing would be "idle" and lack the fundamental requirements for existence.</summary>
<keywords>Aristotle, Ramon Llull, nature, power, operation, essence, active and passive principles, scholasticism, metaphysics</keywords>

->Concerning the Mystery of the Most Holy Trinity.<-

...the reason being that no being can characterize <note>original Spanish: "denominar," meaning to attribute a quality or name to something</note> another unless it is itself of the same nature as the characterization it provides to the other. As **Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher (384–322 BC), whose logic and metaphysics were central to medieval and early modern scholarship</note> indicates in various places, this is a common axiom in Philosophy.

This same Philosopher corroborates our intent in the cited passage, asserting: ***There are three things in any given thing, namely: nature, power, and operation.*** <note>original Latin: "Tria sunt in qualibet re, videlicet natura, virtus, & operatio"</note> There are three things in any thing—namely nature, power, and operation—therefore, in any real essence (which is a "thing"), these three must be found. 

However, **power** <term>virtus: in this context, the inherent capacity or potential for a thing to act or be acted upon</term> is either active or passive; thus, every essence possesses this power, and consequently the **nature** from which it emanates. It follows, then, that the nature of any thing is not possible without this intrinsic power of acting or of **being acted upon** <note>original Spanish: "padecer," which in philosophical terms refers to "passion" or the capacity to receive an action, rather than physical suffering</note>; otherwise, it would fall into a void of purpose caused by its own **inactivity** <note>original Spanish: "ociosidad," implying a state of being "idle" or useless because it lacks the capacity to fulfill its function</note>. 

Therefore, to avoid such an absurdity, it is necessary to concede that in every essence, nature, power, and operation exist not only externally, but also immanently and intrinsically. For otherwise, things would be found to be empty of their own purpose within themselves, lacking the three things which Aristotle says are found in any being or thing.
