# A Defense of Ramon Llull’s Doctrine

<page-num>116</page-num>
### **The Defense of Ramon Llull’s Doctrine**
<meta>catchword: Bo- (likely referring to Bonitas/Goodness on the following page)</meta>

The **Commentator** <note>original Latin: "Commentator." This refers to **Averroes** (Ibn Rushd, 1126–1198), the Andalusian philosopher whose commentaries on Aristotle were foundational to medieval thought</note> also teaches in the 3rd book of the *Metaphysics* that **no thing is granted to be idle.** <note>original Latin: "Nullam rem otiosam dari"</note> Therefore, any essence that is truly a "thing" cannot be idle within itself with respect to itself—that is, intrinsically and through its own **immanent actions** <term>actio immanens: an action that begins and ends within the same being, such as thinking or existing, rather than affecting something external</term>—since, according to **Aristotle** in the cited passage, that power is idle from which no operation proceeds. 

Consequently, if all essences did not possess some intrinsic operation within themselves, they would be idle, and a **void** would exist within them. This is because a void is not merely a lack of physical space, but also a lack of operation and purpose. 

However, this intrinsic operation of essences, and of their active and passive power, can be nothing other than an **immanent action**. This action is produced by the intrinsic forms of essences within their own intrinsic matters, communicating their own being to them. The result is that, by virtue of being what they are, they are able to characterize an external subject by impressing their own likeness upon it, as **Aristotle** notes in the 2nd book of the *Metaphysics*. 

Therefore, in any essence—for example, in **goodness**—the three aforementioned things are found, which **Ramon Llull** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316) was a Majorcan philosopher who developed a complex system of logic using "correlatives" to explain how God and the world function</note> signifies by the suffixes ***-tivum***, ***-bile***, and ***-are***. <note>These represent the active principle (the "doer"), the passive principle (the "receiver"), and the verb/act itself (the "doing"). For example, in Goodness: the *Bonificative*, the *Bonificable*, and the act of *Bonifying*</note> These are...

# Concerning the Mystery of the Most Holy Trinity
<note>original Latin: "circa SSS. Trinit. mysterium."</note>

<page-num>117</page-num>

As the **Commentator** <note>Averroes (Ibn Rushd), the primary medieval commentator on Aristotle</note> also teaches in the 3rd book of the *Metaphysics*, demonstrating that **no thing is found to be idle**; therefore, any essence that is truly a thing cannot be idle within itself with respect to itself—that is, intrinsically, and through its own **immanent actions** <term>immanent actions: activities that remain within the agent, such as thinking or being, rather than moving toward an external object</term>. Because, according to **Aristotle** in the cited passage: "That power is idle from which no operation proceeds." 

Consequently, if all essences did not possess some intrinsic operation within themselves, they would be idle, and a **void** <term>vacuo: the concept of "empty space" or "nothingness," which medieval philosophers argued nature naturally abhors</term> would be found within them. This is because a void is not only a lack of place, but also a lack of operation and of purpose. 

However, this intrinsic operation of essences, and their active and passive virtue, can be nothing other than the **immanent action** produced by the intrinsic forms of essences within their own intrinsic matters, communicating their being to them. In this way, being what they are, they are able to characterize another external subject, impressing their likeness upon it according to **Aristotle** in the 2nd book of the *Metaphysics*. 

Therefore, in any essence—for example, in **Goodness**—there are the three things referred to. These are explained by **Ramon Llull** <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316) was a Majorcan philosopher and mystic known for his "Art," a system using logic and combinations of terms to explain all knowledge, including the Trinity</note> through the suffixes **-tivo**, **-bile**, and **-are**; <note>These are Llull's "correlatives": the active (*-tivus*, e.g., "bonificative"), the passive (*-bilis*, e.g., "bonificable"), and the verb or act itself (*-are*, e.g., "to bonify"). He used these to show that every attribute of God or nature is eternally active within itself.</note>

->**Defense of the Doctrine of Ramon Llull**<-
<note>original: "Propugnatio R. Lul. doctrinæ"</note>

**The good-making power**, **the good-able matter**, and **the act of making good**,
<note>original Latin: *Bonificativum*, *Bonificabile* & *Bonificare*. These are Llull's technical terms for the three components of the essence of Goodness.</note>
the **-making** suffix <note>*-tivum*</note> represents the intrinsic form and active power; the **-able** suffix <note>*-bile*</note> represents the matter and passive power; and the **-ing** suffix <note>*-are*</note> represents the existing intrinsic and immanent operation. Therefore, as the **good-making power** flows into the **good-able matter** by means of the **act of making good**, Goodness results; and any other essence is formed in this same way.

Otherwise, if Goodness did not possess this intrinsic communication within itself—flowing from its **active power** into its **passive potential**—it would be entirely unable to externally "make good" **Greatness**, **Duration**, and so on; nor could any of these essences be able to designate another subject, nor have the power to imprint their own likenesses upon it. It follows, therefore, that Goodness (and likewise the other essences) must have this intrinsic communication within themselves before they are able to share themselves with others. Indeed, it is necessary to admit this intrinsic and immanent operation so that any other external operation may occur; and this exists in every essence to repel **idleness** <note>the state of having a power but not using it</note> and the **void** in nature.

# Defense of the Doctrine of Ramon Lull <note>Ramon Lull (c. 1232–1316) was a philosopher and mystic from Majorca who developed a complex logical system, known as the "Art," to prove Christian truths through reason.</note> #

...to safely work out this intrinsic and extrinsic constitution of all created things, I assert that we are capable. This is proven in the following way:

In neither the divine nor the human nature is it permitted to imagine or consider more than four things. These are: **being** <note>original: *esse*</note>, its **likeness**, **non-being** <note>original: *non esse*</note>, and its **likeness**. 

However, **non-being** does not exist, nor can it be thought of; therefore, neither can its likeness exist. In both the uncreated <note>A philosophical term for God, who exists of Himself and was not made.</note> and the created, therefore, only **being** and its likeness remain. 

But God is **Being**: therefore, every created thing is His **likeness**; or let something else be demonstrated that bears this characteristic. Therefore, in every created thing, a representation of the uncreated exists. 

Consequently, no perfection can be present in the created thing (which is the likeness) that is not found with greater excellence in the one being likened—namely, God (otherwise it would not be His likeness). Indeed, no thing can exist that is not representative of that which really co-exists in Him. Nor is there anything thinkable in this supreme **Being** whose likeness is not represented in creatures (or it would be a dissimilarity). Therefore, as a likeness...

-> ### Concerning the mystery of the Most Holy Trinity <note>original: *circa SSS. Trinit. mysterium.* SSS is an abbreviation for *Santísima* or *Sacratísima* (Most Holy).</note> ### <-

...of the intrinsic and extrinsic constitution of all created things, which is proven in this manner:

In all of divine nature, nor in human nature, are more than four things possible, or can they even be considered: namely, **being**, its **likeness**, **non-being**, and its **likeness**.

But **non-being** is neither possible nor can it be considered; therefore, neither can its likeness be. There remain only **being** and its **likeness** in all that is uncreated <note>A theological term for God, who exists by His own nature rather than being made by another.</note> and created.

Now then, **Being** is God; therefore, His **likeness** is everything created—or let some other thing be identified that is so. Consequently, in everything created, there is a representation of the uncreated.

Therefore, no perfection can be found in the created thing (which is the likeness) that is not found more perfectly in the one being likened, which is God (otherwise it would not be His likeness); nor is there any thing that is not representative of that which really exists in Him. Nor in this supreme **Being** is anything found that is not represented in the creature, His likeness (or it would be His dissimilarity). Therefore, in order to—

<meta>catchword: tum</meta>

This is proven by the minor premise <note>In a logical syllogism, the "minor premise" is the second statement that links a specific case to a general rule.</note>. According to all sound Philosophy, it is more perfect to exist and to act at the same time than to exist alone: therefore, so that this perfection is not lacking in God, it is necessary that in Him, from all eternity and through all eternity, the "producing" and the "produced" are present. 

Otherwise, **God** would not be God; for He would not be perfect, except only in *existence*, and not at all in *action*; nor would He exist as a <term>pure act</term> <note>original: *actus purus*; a Scholastic term for a being that is fully realized and active, with no unfulfilled potential or "idleness."</note>, seeing as He would have been occupied with idleness through all His eternity. Nor would He be infinite, because in Himself He would not be everything that He is and everything that He can be; He would only possess the potential to act within time, which would make His power finite and incompatible with the **divine essence**. 

For this reason, God would not be infinitely distant from nothingness in His entirety and through all His parts; indeed, a deficiency of "sterility" would be recognized in Him, since He would be unfruitful in Himself and through Himself. Therefore, the uncreated being...

...to grant in all His eternity, and throughout His eternity, everything that is most perfect, as has been proven. Now then, the fact that there is a **producer** and a **produced** is what is most perfect; therefore, the conclusion follows.

I prove the minor premise <note>In a logical argument or syllogism, the "minor premise" is the second statement that links a specific case to a general rule.</note>. According to all sound **Philosophy**, it is more perfect to exist and to act jointly than to exist alone: therefore, so that this perfection may be in God, it is necessary that within Him there is the producer and the produced from all His eternity and through all His eternity.

Otherwise, **God** would not be God, because He would not be perfect, except only in *existence*, and in no way in *action*. Nor would He be a <term>pure act</term> <note>original: *acto puro*; a technical term in Scholastic philosophy for a being that is fully realized and eternally active, having no unfulfilled potential or "idleness."</note>, as He would have been idle through all His eternity; nor would He be infinite, because He would not be in Himself everything that He is and everything that He can be. He would only be a potentiality to act in time, and thus finite and incompatible with a **divine essence**. 

Consequently, God would not be infinitely distant from nothingness in all things and for all things, because the deficiency of sterility <note>The author is arguing that if God did not "produce" the other persons of the Trinity within Himself, He would be "sterile" or unfruitful, which he considers a flaw.</note> would be found in Him, He being unfruitful in Himself and by Himself, etc. Thus, it is evident that the being...

# Defense of the doctrine of Ramon Llull <note>original: *Propugnatio R. Lul. doctrinæ*. Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1316) was a Majorcan philosopher and theologian who developed a complex logical system to prove the tenets of Christian faith, including the Trinity, through reason.</note>
<page-type>text</page-type>

And this is because that being is more noble and perfect which is good and produces good; which is infinite and produces an infinite thing; which is eternal and produces an eternal thing; which is almighty and produces an almighty thing, etc., than that being which is good, infinite, eternal, and almighty, but does not produce the same. Therefore, **God**, who is good, infinite, eternal, etc., produces that which is good, infinite, and so forth. 

This is so that He may exist not only as more perfect than every other being, but as absolutely the most perfect and most noble of all. Otherwise, it would follow that <term>privation</term> <note>original: *privationem*; the absence or lack of a quality that should naturally be present.</note> and potentiality are more noble than act itself, which is repugnant to all reason. 

Therefore, so that all these inconsistencies may be turned away from God and denied within His divine being, we are bound by a **compelling necessity** <note>original: *necessitate necessitante*; a philosophical term for an absolute or "necessitating" necessity that leaves no other logical alternative.</note> to admit within Him a **producer** and a **produced**; and consequently, that from all His eternity He produces the good, the great, the infinite, the eternal, etc. Or else, God [would be inferior] to all creatures...

### Defense of the Doctrine of Ramon Lull

...a plurality of persons cannot be denied.

This is clearly shown by **Ramon Lull** <note>Ramon Lull (c. 1232–1315) was a philosopher and mystic from Majorca who sought to prove Christian doctrines using a system of logic known as his "Art."</note> (and all our Doctors of the Church confirm it) because **God**, in understanding Himself, through the power of His own act of understanding, perceives Himself as all-powerful and full of all perfections, and consequently as fruitful. Furthermore, since God has known Himself from all His eternity, it is necessary that He produce the Word from all His eternity. Otherwise, His intellect would have existed throughout all eternity as idle, and consequently empty of purpose (and thus more imperfect than all other created beings). Therefore, it follows almost as a matter of evidence that there is a "generation of the word" and a plurality of persons within the divine nature.

And neither fewer nor more, as **Ramon Llull** <note>A 13th-century Majorcan philosopher and theologian known for his logical "Art" designed to prove the truths of Christianity.</note> proves. For it is incomprehensible that there should be more; a fourth would either have to be the <term>Good-maker</term> <note>original: *bonificante*; Llull’s specific term for the active principle of Goodness, associated with the Father.</note>, which is the Father; or the <term>Good-capable</term> and <term>Good-made</term> <note>original: *bonificable y bonificado*; Llull’s terms for that which can receive goodness and is produced as Goodness, associated with the Son.</note>, which is the Son; or the act of <term>Good-making</term> <note>original: *el bonificar*; Llull’s term for the actualizing bond or movement of goodness, associated with the Holy Spirit.</note>. To suggest there are two Good-makers or two Fathers, two Good-capables or two Sons, or two Good-makings and two Holy Spirits, is contrary to divine perfection.

# Defense of the Doctrine of Ramon Llull

[conti]nuous, and even less can <term>discrete quantity<note>In traditional philosophy, "discrete quantity" refers to things that are separate and can be counted as distinct units, like people in a room, as opposed to "continuous quantity" like a line or a flow of water.</note></term> exist [in God], because this is only found among things of different essences and subjects. It is like a human being, who is composed of soul, body, and their union; these exist as diverse units. And therefore, in a human, an absolute and <term>categorical number<note>original: "numerus absolutus & prædicamentalis". This refers to the logical category of "Quantity" in Aristotelian thought, which applies to physical things but not to the simple nature of God.</note></term> is admitted. This is also the case between Peter and his horse, which are of different essence; or among diverse concrete things, such as in "created goodness" <note>Llull distinguishes between "created goodness" (the goodness we see in the world) and "uncreated goodness" (God himself).</note>, where there is a **bonificative** [that which makes good], a **bonificable** [that which can be made good], and the act of **bonifying** [the making good]; in this case, one is not the essence of the other.

And although these three constitute the essence of goodness, nevertheless none of them is goodness by itself; rather, all three together are. Nor is goodness any one of them individually, and therefore in created goodness, three units and an absolute number are admitted. However, in God, goodness is essentially the **bonificative**, the **bonificable**, and the **act of bonifying** <note>original: "bonificativum, bonificabile, & bonificare". These are Llull’s signature terms used to explain the Trinity: the Father (active/bonificative), the Son (passive/bonificable), and the Holy Spirit (the act/bonifying).</note>, and each of these exists as the goodness itself, and the goodness is each of them. For this reason, they by no means constitute diverse units, but only one unit and substance, which is goodness itself. Thus the Father is the whole essence, and the Son is the whole es-

<summary>This page explains Ramon Llull’s philosophical defense of the Trinity, arguing that while created things can be divided into separate parts, in God, the different functional roles (the Maker, the Made, and the Act) are all a single, unified essence.</summary>

And although these three are constitutive of the essence of goodness, none of them is the goodness itself; rather, all three together are. Nor is the goodness any one of them individually. And thus, within created goodness, three units and an absolute triple number are found. 

But in God, goodness is essentially the **bonificative**, the **bonificable**, and the **bonifying**, and each one of them is the goodness, and the goodness is each one of them: for which reason they do not constitute diverse units, but one single unity, which is the goodness. <meta>This page continues the theological defense of Raymond Lull’s (1232–1315) teachings on the Trinity. ### Defense of the Doctrine of Raymond Lull

**168**

[...the Father is the whole] essence, and the **Holy Spirit** is also the whole essence; and consequently, those three exist as one and the same divine essence and nature. Therefore, in Divine matters, an **absolute** or **categorical number** <note>original: "numerus absolutus vel prædicamentalis." In Aristotelian logic, these are numbers used to count physical things in the category of "Quantity."</note> is not granted; although a **transcendental number**, or a "description of number," is admitted. This is because distinct units are not found in them **substantively** <note>Meaning they are not separate "substances" or individual beings like three separate men would be.</note>, which would be required to establish an absolute or categorical number. 

Thus, a description of number or a **transcendental number** <note>A philosophical term used to count things that are not physical, such as the distinct "Persons" within the single "Being" of God.</note> is accepted; because we count **three acts**, or **three personal properties**, or **three really distinct relations**. For although four relations are given, nevertheless that of **active spiration** <term>Active Spiration<note>The theological term for the "breathing forth" of the Holy Spirit by the Father and the Son together.</note></term> does not constitute a "person": because it is not relatively opposed either to the Father or to the Son.

From this it is recognized that the Respondent involves himself in the very objection he proposed. For although it is true that **discrete quantity** <note>Counting separate, individual items (1, 2, 3).</note> consists of distinct units from which binary [two] and ternary [three] numbers are formed, it is nevertheless false that it is irrelevant whether their essences are one or many, toward the unit[ies...]

It clarifies that while there are four theological relations in God, only three constitute distinct "Persons" (Father, Son, and Holy Spirit).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical defense of Ramon Llull’s view on the Trinity against an opponent (the "Respondent"). It focuses on how we can count "three" in God without implying that God is made of three separate pieces or "quantities."</meta>

# Defense of Ramon Llull’s Doctrine

...convey the meaning: thus it denotes the unity of essence and three Persons, provided that "unity" is understood as a substance <term>substantively; referring to what something is in itself</term>. For if we take it as a quality <term>adjectivally; describing a property</term>, we might not improperly say there are "three unities." For the divine nature *as such* <note>original Latin: "ut sic"</note> involves no plurality at all. However, when understood *notionally* <note>original: "notionaliter" — a technical term referring to the specific characteristics or "notions" that allow us to distinguish the Father, Son, and Spirit</note> and according to these notions, it possesses three personal properties. These properties, which constitute the three Persons, are of one and the same nature and essence (as the Respondent admits), even though the Persons themselves are truly distinct from one another.

Indeed, for this reason, three unities understood substantively do not constitute a "three" in the sense of a predicamental <term>predicamental number; a number used to count physical, separate objects in the world</term> number. As previously stated, for such a number to exist, there would have to be a distinction between multiple essences, or between essences and their individual subjects <term>supposita; the underlying "who" or "what" that possesses a nature</term>. Therefore, the Respondent's attempt to establish "discrete quantity" <note>a mathematical concept where units are distinct and separate, like three stones</note> within divine matters is entirely false.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Furthermore, we deny the claim that one cannot speak of three Persons without implying the coexistence of "quantity." When we profess "three," we are counting the acts of divine production <note>this refers to the "processions" within the Trinity: the Father bringing forth the Son, and the Holy Spirit proceeding from the Father and Son</note>. These acts create neither an "absolute" number nor a "predicamental" one, because they are all of the very same essence.

On the contrary, this negation <note>the denial of physical parts</note> argues for the perfection of divine matters, as has been explained. # Defense of the doctrine of Ramon Llull <note>original: *Propugnatio R. Lul. doctrinæ*</note>

<column-break/>

...states. And the Lord Respondent is not unaware that actions belong to individuals <note>original: *actiones sunt suppositorum*; a principle in scholastic philosophy meaning that actions are performed by a specific "person" or "subject" rather than by an abstract nature</note>, and for this reason I pray that he reread the text without distortion, for he will find it more clearly explained where Ramon Llull adds below: *“Of which each is of such a kind, each one remaining essentially distinct, and each is of such a kind through generation and spiration <term>spiration; from the Latin "spiratio" (breathing), the theological term for the way the Holy Spirit "proceeds" from the Father and the Son</term>, in which exists the distinction of persons.”* Ramon Llull is unable to reveal the matter more clearly, nor can he explain it in a more orthodox <note>conforming to the accepted or traditional faith</note> manner.

To the 5th objection (which the Respondent raises against the argument that Ramon Llull deduces), I respond: This Most Eminent Master did not wish to use the terms and comparisons most common in the schools <note>referring to the university "Scholastic" environment dominated by followers of Thomas Aquinas or Duns Scotus</note>—because those are only suitable for people well-versed in them—but chose instead to use only common terms and natural examples, so that those ignorant of the sciences might be able to understand. Therefore, he teaches that God is "one" in two ways. ...magnifies through goodness. Each principle concerns its own proper act: just as justice and mercy are God Himself, yet an act of justice is nonetheless distinguished from an act of mercy. But since all these principles are God Himself, and this "act" is most pure, it is necessary that they all coexist as active and productive. Otherwise, they would have to be judged as mere potentiality; if that were the case, a "composition" <term>composition: the philosophical error of suggesting God is made of separate parts, rather than being a single, simple essence.</term> would be granted within God. Then, two contradictory things would be verified at once, and this divine *being* would not remain the most perfect being.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Nor did Ramon Llull teach that these principles are the origins of God. For His *being* is without origin and lacks a beginning. <page-num>214</page-num>

<header>Defense of Ramon Llull’s Doctrine</header>

For a thing to be a principle <note>original: *principium*</note>, any kind of priority is sufficient for it; such as the priority which the Father has over the Son, and which both have over the Holy Spirit. This priority consists only in origin, not in time or in nature <note>The author is clarifying that in the Trinity, the Father "comes first" only in the sense that he is the source, not that he existed before the Son.</note>.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;From this, it follows that this objection, repeated so many times, is utterly useless; and it is even more so to assert that a "productive principle" that is not also an "efficient cause" <term>efficient cause: in Aristotelian philosophy, the agent that brings something into being. The author argues that God's internal workings are "productive" without being "causes" in the way a builder causes a house.</term> cannot be found. For we have proven the opposite, and philosophy teaches this universally: demonstrating that the categories <term>categories: from the Latin *praedicamenta*, referring to the fundamental ways of classifying being.</term> are principles and not efficient causes, as we showed in the cited introductory work on page 23. The Respondent <note>Respondent: the critic or opponent in this theological debate.</note> could have examined this before doubting whether such a proposition should be embraced by everyone as most true. It was also explained there that Ramon Llull called these reasons <term>reasons: from the Latin *rationes*, the distinct logical aspects of God's single essence.</term> "principles" either because they are the primitive elements of his Art <note>The "Art" refers to Llull’s *Ars Magna*, a logical system using combinations of divine attributes to reach universal truths.</note>, or because he did not judge other terms or words to be more suitable for specifying their essences. Indeed, even all theologians admit within God a "principle which" <note>original: *principium quod* — refers to the Person who acts (e.g., the Father).</note>, and a "principle by which" <note>original: *principium quo* — refers to the attribute or power by which the person acts.</note>, where neither an external effect nor a cause is granted.

Likewise, all theologians admit the "principle which" <note>original: *Principio quod*; the subject that acts.</note> and the "principle by which" <note>original: *Principio quo*; the power or attribute by which the subject acts.</note> in God, in a context where there is neither effect nor cause.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;Thus, in response to the 12th objection, it is answered that we have already stated that these "reasons" <note>original: *raçones* (Latin: *rationes*). For Llull, this triad in nature is a reflection of the Holy Trinity.</term> (and the same is true of heat). And if heat produces its likeness separate from itself, it is because its activity is greater than that of whiteness.

But since goodness is the "reason" (as has been proven) why that which is good produces that which is good, and eternity is why it produces that which is eternal, and unity why it produces that which is one, it is necessary that God produces that which is good, one, and eternal—which is his Son <note>This refers to the "Necessary Reasons" argument, where Lull argues that God’s goodness must, by its very nature, communicate itself perfectly, resulting in the generation of the Son within the Trinity.</note>. # Defense of the doctrine of R. Llull

fection, essence, and the nature of these reasons <term>reasons (rationes): in Llull’s system, these are the "Dignities" or attributes of God, such as Goodness, Power, and Wisdom.</term> consists in acting; otherwise they would be idle, and thus more imperfect than created beings, and would not stand as the most perfect of all beings. Nor, for this reason, is God divisible; no divisibility can be imagined in his divine *being*.

Regarding the 15th objection, it is responded by granting that the acts of one attribute or reason are not attributed to another. However, we deny that the divine reasons and attributes are distinguished only by the "reasoning reason" <term>reasoning reason (ratio ratiocinans): a distinction made only in the mind of the observer, without a basis in the thing being studied.</term>. Nevertheless, if he understands the distinction of the "reasoned reason" <term>reasoned reason (ratio ratiocinata): a distinction that, while not dividing the essence of a thing, is based on a real foundation within the nature of that thing.</term>—whether with a foundation in the thing itself, from the nature of the thing, or virtually—then we judge it to be so. And Ramon Llull himself embraces this very view. 

We also judge it to be most true that the intellect can take nothing away from, nor really add anything to, the essence of God. But the intellect would take away a great deal from Him if it denied (as the Respondent does) that the divine intellect produces anything—namely, God himself as the one understanding, the Will as the one loving the product, and the product reciprocally producing <note>original: *se ipsum Deum intelligentem, & voluntatem productum amantem, & productum reciprocè producentem*. This refers to the internal processions of the Trinity: the Father (Intellect) begetting the Son (Product/Word) and the Spirit (Will/Love) proceeding from them.</note>. For in this way, he would remove all activity and perfection from God, and deprive Him of his very being.

<meta>This page continues the defense of Ramon Llull’s theology, specifically refuting the idea that the distinctions between God’s attributes (like intellect and will) are merely mental constructs. It builds on the previous page's argument that these "reasons" or "Dignities" must have a real foundation in God's nature to avoid making God's essence appear idle or imperfect.</meta>

### Defense of the doctrine of Ramon Llull <note>original: *Propugnatio R. Lul. doctrinæ*</note>

<meta>catchword: ef-</meta>

No Catholic denies that the intellect, the will, and the other attributes and reasons <term>reasons (*rationes*): in Llull's thought, these are the "Dignities" or active divine attributes like Goodness, Greatness, and Power.</term> are identified within the essence of God (for they exist as the very essence of God itself). Nor does any Catholic deny that all those things which do not imply a relative opposition <note>relative opposition: a theological term meaning the only things truly "distinct" in God are the relationships between the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit.</note> are identified within that essence. This does not mean that anything is added to or taken away from the divine essence because of these distinctions; for the essence itself contains and encompasses all things within itself.

However, we reject the claim that the will, the intellect, and the other reasons and attributes are diversified *only* by the "reasoning reason" <term>reasoning reason (*ratio ratiocinans*): a distinction made entirely by the human mind that does not exist in the object being studied.</term>. We also reject the idea that the personal properties <note>original: *proprietates personales*</note> are not really distinguished among themselves, and consequently, we reject the claim that the three divine Persons are not really distinguished.

Furthermore, we deny that the following conclusion is valid: namely, the claim that if the intellect and will were not "productive" <note>In Llull's system, "productive" means that the attributes must act (the Intellect must understand, the Will must love) for God to be perfect.</note>, they would be lesser than His omnipotence (and so would all the divine reasons). If that were true, then "more" and "less," or "superiority" and "inferiority"—rather than the highest equality—would be introduced into God <note>original: *in Deū*</note>, and consequently, imperfection and defect would follow. Nor does this argument mean we wish to suppose that these things are distinct from the essence of God (for they are all the essence itself)...

<summary>The author argues that God’s attributes (like Will and Intellect) must be more than just mental concepts; they must have a real foundation in His nature to maintain the equality of His powers and the reality of the Trinity.</summary>

<keywords>Ramon Llull, Divine Essence, Trinity, Divine Attributes, Reasoned Reason, Divine Equality, Omnipotence</keywords>

### -> Concerning the mystery of the Most Holy Trinity <-
<note>original Latin header: *circa SSS. Trinit. mysterium*</note>

<meta>catchword: to- (completing "todos")</meta>

*There is no Catholic who denies that the intellect, the will, and all the other attributes and reasons* <term>reasons (*rationes*): In the Lullian tradition, these are the "Dignities of God"—active, divine attributes like Goodness, Greatness, and Power that are both the essence of God and the principles of creation.</term> *are identified within the same essence; for they are the same divine essence. And likewise, no Catholic denies that everything which does not imply relative opposition* <note>relative opposition: A technical term in Trinitarian theology meaning that the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are only distinguished by their relationships to one another (e.g., begetting vs. being begotten), not by their essence.</note> *is identified within that essence among itself, without anything being added to or taken away from the divine essence because of this; for the essence includes all things within itself.*

*But we deny that the will, the intellect, and all the other reasons and attributes are distinguished only by our reasoning intellect* <term>reasoning intellect (*entendimiento raciocinante*): A reference to the "ratio ratiocinans," where a distinction exists only in the mind of the observer and not in the reality of the object itself.</term>. *And we deny that the personal properties are not really distinguished among themselves, and consequently, that the three divine Persons are not really distinguished.*

*Furthermore, we deny that the following is not a very sound conclusion: that if the intellect and will were not productive* <note>productive: Referring to the internal "processions" of the Trinity—the Intellect "producing" the Son (the Word) and the Will "producing" the Holy Spirit (Love). Llull argued these must be active powers.</note>, *they would be lesser than omnipotence; and consequently, so would all the divine reasons. Because in that case, there would be a "more" and a "less," or a majority and a minority, rather than supreme equality; and thus, there would be imperfection and defect. Nor does this argument suppose that these things are distinct from the essence of God; because...*

<summary>This page argues that God's attributes (Intellect, Will, etc.) must have a real, active basis within His essence rather than being mere mental concepts. The author contends that if these attributes were not "productive" (active in the Trinity), they would be inferior to God's Power, destroying the perfect equality and simplicity of the divine nature.</summary>

# Defense of Ramon Llull’s Doctrine #

In the 18th objection, the Respondent <note>The "Respondent" refers to a critic of Llull, likely a scholastic theologian who challenged Llull's use of non-traditional terminology.</note> reasons from no firmer foundation regarding created objects and power than he does regarding uncreated [divine] ones; or regarding metaphysical objects and power as if they were logical ones; or regarding real beings as if they were <term>beings of reason</term> <note>Beings of reason (*entia rationis*): Concepts that exist only in the mind (like "blindness" or "nothingness"), as opposed to "real beings" which have independent existence.</note>. 

In uncreated things, the object is not [prior] to the power; on the contrary, the power is presupposed to the object only by a certain <term>priority of origin</term> <note>Priority of origin: A theological distinction used to explain the Trinity. It suggests that while the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are co-eternal (none is older than the other), the Father is the "source" or "origin" of the others in a logical sense.</note>. Because it is impossible for a being to be understood as "notionally" <term>intelligizing</term> <note>original: *intelligens*. The active power of understanding.</note> and **unificative**, etc. (which is how the power exists), and as **intelligible** and **unificable**, etc. (which is the object), without some priority being admitted. This is not a priority of time, nor of nature; therefore, it is a priority of origin. This is because it is necessary that we conceive of the Father before we conceive of the Son.

In almost this same way, metaphysical power and objects, and the internal <term>concretes</term> <note>Concretes: In Llull’s "Art," these are the functional components of a substance, such as the power to act and the capacity to be acted upon.</note> of all created things—as is the case for all "actives" <note>original: *tivorum*. Referring to Llull's use of the *-tivus* suffix to denote active principles, such as *bonificativus* (good-making).</note> in respect to their "passives" <note>original: *bile*. Referring to the *-bilis* suffix for passive capacities, such as *bonificabilis* (capable of being made good).</note>—must be understood. 

However, in these [created] things, a **priority of nature** results. For the **bonificative** <note>The active principle of goodness.</note> comes to the mind before the **bonificable** <note>The capacity to receive or be goodness.</note>, and it is understood in nature just like a **form** <note>In Aristotelian philosophy, the "form" is the active principle that gives a thing its essential characteristics.</note>, or that which is more perfect and [closest] in likeness to God...

### Concerning the Mystery of the Most Holy Trinity <note>original Latin: *circa SSS. Trinit. mysterium.*</note> ###

It suggests that while the Father, Son, and Holy Spirit are co-eternal and equal, the Father is logically the "source" or "origin" of the Son.</note> only. This is not a priority of time, nor of nature; therefore, it is a priority of origin, because it is necessary that we conceive of the Father before the Son.

In almost this same way are the metaphysical and internal powers and objects of all created things, such as all the **active principles** <note>original: *tivos*. Llull uses the suffix *-bile* (e.g., *bonificable*) to represent the capacity to receive the action of that attribute.</note>. However, in created things, their priority is one of nature: because in nature, the **bonificative** <note>The active power of "Goodness" to communicate itself.</note> is understood before the **bonificable** <note>That which is capable of being made good.</note>, just as form is prior [to matter], being more perfect and more similar to God. # Defense of the doctrine of Ramon Llull #

...receives [the object] into its own intrinsic intelligible nature, or its **possible intellect** <note>original: *intellectu possibili*. In medieval philosophy, this refers to the human mind's capacity to be shaped by new information and form concepts.</note>, and brings forth an expressed likeness; this is the "production of the thing understood," or the **Word** <note>original: *Verbum*. The author uses the human process of forming a concept as an analogy for the eternal generation of the Son (the Word) by God the Father.</note>: and the same happens in its own way regarding the will.

However, the **Respondent** <note>The "Respondent" refers to a specific critic or opponent who has challenged Llull’s teachings in a formal debate or text.</note> seems to close his eyes to all philosophical truths. Indeed, by denying these principles of Ramon Llull, he supposes that in both **uncreated and created** <term>increatis atque creatis</term> <note>In this context, "uncreated" refers to God, while "created" refers to humans and the physical universe.</note> things, wisdom is exactly the same as the intellect.

Yet, according to the best and truest way of philosophizing, it is clear to everyone that in created beings, "wisdom" exists only as a certain **habit** <note>original: *habitum*. Here meaning a stable quality or "disposition" that the mind acquires through learning, rather than an essential part of its nature.</note> of the intellect, as I explained in the first pages of my *Introductory* text. Nevertheless, in uncreated things [the Divine], God is identical to His own intellect—which is God Himself. Because of His goodness, infinity, unity, and other attributes (which coexist as that same divine intellect), He is productive and **fruitful** <note>original: *fœcundus*. A theological term meaning God is not "static" but eternally active in the internal relationships of the Trinity.</note>.

Therefore, wisdom, by reason of its own richness, begets wisdom (which is God Himself, as has been so often repeated). But the lights of wisdom [do not shine] upon intellects that fight against the truth and intend to obscure it with reproaches...

And if **Saint Thomas** <note>Thomas Aquinas (1225–1274), the Dominican friar and "Angelic Doctor" whose synthesis of Aristotle and Christianity dominated medieval university life.</note> denied them in any particular passage, in others he explains himself and admits them, considering them necessary—just as all who are grounded in true philosophy agree. Indeed, these [species] are consistent with the experience we have drawn from all natural beings and from their perpetual way of operating—namely, by projecting their **likenesses** outside of themselves so that they may be represented in water, in mirrors, and in the "crystals" <note>An early term for the lens of the eye.</note> of our eyes. # Defense of the doctrine of Ramon Llull
<note>original: *Propugnatio R. Lul. doctrinæ*</note>

...likeness and unity of nature.

R. Llull and our most holy Doctors <note>A title referring to authoritative Christian theologians, such as Thomas Aquinas or Bonaventure, who established the standard "Scholastic" definitions of the Trinity.</note> assert that the Son is called such and is most properly "begotten" <note>In theology, "generation" or "being begotten" is the specific term for the origin of the Son from the Father.</note>. This follows from the fact that the Son proceeds through **intellection**, and the Holy Spirit proceeds through **volition** <term>volition: the act of willing or loving; the movement of the heart toward a desired object</term> or the will. This is the case when the one who understands understands himself; that which proceeds through intellection, by the power of its own process, proceeds as something similar to the one understanding. For the intellect, by its very nature, draws to itself a likeness of that which it understands. 

And since God understands Himself, He draws His own likeness; and this intellection has the **Inner Word** <note>original: *Verbum interius*; a technical term for a concept or thought before it is spoken aloud. In this context, it refers to the Son as the "Logos" or wisdom of God.</note>, which it expresses, as its goal, which exists as the "species" or likeness of the thing understood. 

But that which proceeds through loving—because the will, by its own nature, does not draw the likeness of the beloved to itself, but only projects its own inclination toward the beloved—is not formally similar to the beloved by the power of its process, even though the lover loves himself. For willing or loving remains an action of the will, which does not express itself through itself, nor does it state any likeness, but (as...

<summary>This page explains the Lullian and Scholastic distinction between the Son and the Holy Spirit: the Son is a "likeness" of the Father because He is produced by God's self-understanding (intellect), whereas the Holy Spirit is an "inclination" because He is produced by God's self-love (will).</summary>

This is because the intellect, by its very nature, draws to itself the likeness of what it understands. Since God understands Himself, He draws His own likeness; and this act of understanding has as its end the **Inner Word** <note>original: *Verbo interior*; in theology, this refers to the "Logos," the perfect mental concept God has of Himself, which is the Son.</note> which it expresses. This Word is the **species** <term>species: a technical term for the mental image or representation of an object in the mind</term> or likeness of the thing understood. 

However, that which proceeds through the act of wanting is different. Because the **will**, by its very nature, does not draw the likeness of the beloved to itself, but rather casts all its inclination toward the object, it is not "formally" similar to the one who wants by the mere power of its process (because there is no "species" or mental image formed there). This remains true even if the one who wants is wanting and loving himself; because wanting is an action of the will, which in itself does not express or state any likeness. He is the Word, or the species, or the expressed image of the Father himself, coexisting with Him. 

And this is the reason why this process or production is called **generation**. 

But the Spirit, who proceeds through the act of loving, is said to be similar to his principle <note>original: *principio*; in this context, the source or origin from which a person of the Trinity proceeds</note> (from whom he proceeds) not formally <note>In Scholastic thought, "formal" similarity means sharing the same form or likeness, as a son looks like a father.</note>, but only identically and materially. This means that this "term" <note>original: *terminus*; the end result of a divine process</note> exists as a product of the mutual love of the Father and the Son, and is identified with the divine essence. 

However, all the **Doctors** and **Classic authors** whom the Response cites admit the impossibility of knowing this difference through the external senses—as Arius <note>Arius (c. 256–336 AD) was a priest whose teachings led to Arianism, which argued that the Son was a created being and not co-eternal or of the same substance as God the Father.</note> intended. Yet, they by no means denied that such knowledge can be attained through the medium of **faith**, in the manner we have explained.

# Defense of the Doctrine of Ramon Llull
<note>original: *Propugnatio R. Lul. doctrinæ*</note>

21st. The response is clear from what was stated previously: and therefore, I only ask the Respondent to point out to me the passage in which Ramon Llull attributes or assigns the same operations to the intellect as he does to the divine will. For it is utterly contrary to justice to quote someone falsely and to argue against an author concerning a matter he never even imagined. 

Proceeding then to his antecedent <note>in logic, the "if" part of an "if-then" statement</note>, because it is entirely false, I deny it. Furthermore, I deny what he supposes within it—namely, that the divine intellect would not be free. For although the intellect necessarily produces the Son on account of its infinite perfection, and both <note>the Father and the Son</note> breathe forth the Holy Spirit, it is no less free than the will. For the will necessarily breathes forth the third person through the act of loving; and yet, it does not lose its freedom because of this, nor does it cease to be of the same essence as the intellect. But truly, in respect to created beings, what is it that has stripped the divine intellect of its freedom?

The consequent <note>the "then" part of a logical proposition</note>, being deduced without order and not in a syllogistic manner <note>a formal argument consisting of a major premise, a minor premise, and a conclusion</note>, is also denied. is <note>original: *est*; this completes the sentence from the previous page regarding the Holy Spirit "in the way of spiration."</note>, if one [attribute] did not unify another, God would stand as defective; for in Him a majority of certain reasons <term>reasons: *rationes*; in this context, the term refers to the distinct aspects, attributes, or "Dignities" of God, such as Goodness, Greatness, and Eternity</term> and a minority of others would be found. Consequently, He would be stripped of His attribute and the principle of supreme equality, which is the highest imperfection. It follows, therefore, since this divine Lord is most fecund <term>fecund: *fœcundissimus*; meaning supremely fruitful or productive. In Lullian theology, this refers to God's inherent nature to act and communicate His essence internally as the Trinity</term>, that everything found within Him must necessarily exist in such a way. 

And consequently, goodness, unity, etc., should not be seen as mere concepts or forms fabricated by our intellect, but must be most real. This remains true even if the distinction between them is carried out through the operation of our "reasoned reason" <note>original: *rationis ratiocinatæ*; a scholastic term referring to the mind's ability to analyze an object after it has been perceived</note>, either with a *foundation in reality* <note>original: *cum fundamento in re*; a technical phrase meaning that while the mind makes a distinction, there is a real basis for it in the object itself</note>, or (as the **doctrine of Ramon Llull** holds) *according to the nature of the thing—that is, it follows from the nature of the thing itself.* 

For if the distinction between God’s reasons were molded by our imagining intellect through "reasoning reason" <note>original: *ratione ratiocinante*; the active power of the mind to create categories or abstractions</note>, it would follow that the intellect is greater than God Himself. This is because the intellect would be powerful enough to imagine perfections that were supposed to exist in the Divine **Being** <note>original: *esse Divino*</note>, yet they would not actually coexist in Him except through our imagination. This is completely absurd; and to [heavenly] spirits—

However, the response proposed here goes further: since it moves from operations directed inward to those directed outward, between which an infinite difference lies. For from these outward operations, the titles of **Creator**, **Just**, and so on, result in God; but from those inward operations produced by all His reasons <term>reasons: *rationes*; Llull’s term for the "Dignities" or primary attributes of God, such as Goodness, Greatness, and Power</term>, no such distinction results. This is because God Himself, and the same divine essence and nature, coexist—since in Him it is impossible to touch one "reason" without all of them being touched at once.

For, as has been deduced, everything that exists in God is God and His essence; and since His nature is most fruitful <note>original: "fœcundissima"; Llull argues that God is not a static being but is inherently active and "fecund," eternally producing within Himself</note>, everything that exists within it must be of such a kind. And since His omnipotence and perfection are the principle of this fecundity (which belongs to His very intellect), by understanding Himself, He generates the **Word** <note>original: "Verbum"; the second person of the Trinity, the Son or Logos</note>. And since this "act of understanding" cannot exist without the acts of the other divine reasons (as we have proved)—because they are the same essence, nature, and substance—the act of understanding occurs alongside **good-making** and **one-making**... <note>original: "bonificare, unificare"; Llull uses these active participles to describe God’s internal life as a constant state of "doing" His attributes</note>

...the mixture of [the composition], and by elucidating the greater notions; to which the human intellect—in its investigation of the operations of a provident nature, to uncover her secrets and to philosophize upon the most solid foundations—is able to arrive? And can it [the intellect] reach the knowledge of the one primeval principle through these means (as **Aristotle**, **Socrates**, and others achieved; and also the fact that this principle possesses a divine fecundity <note>The "fertility" or capacity of God to produce/beget within the Trinity.</note>, as **Trismegistus** <note>Referring to Hermes Trismegistus, a legendary Hellenistic figure associated with wisdom and alchemy.</note> indicates in his *Pimander* <note>A foundational text of Hermeticism.</note> with these words: *The Monad begat the Monad, etc.* <note>original Latin: "Monas genuit Monadem." In this context, it suggests the Father (the One) begetting the Son (the One).</note> and in other places)? To what greater height of wisdom could the scrutiny of the **Pagan Philosophers** ascend? Indeed, through <term>sublimatories: high-level intellectual or spiritual processes, likened to the alchemical process of "sublimation" where a substance is purified and elevated</term>, they recognized that which is now refused by those who were bound to believe it by their own law and Sacred testimonies.

Does the Lord Respondent think that **Socrates**, **Aristotle**, and other distinguished Philosophers did not speak allegorically and figuratively in their philosophy, concealing the production of the "greater creature" (which is found among corporeal things) with their applications and metaphors? For this reason, they have remained so poorly untangled or understood by many; and these [critics], pleasing themselves only with the literal sense <note>original: "sensu positivo." Referring to a surface-level or "positive" reading that lacks depth or allegorical understanding.</note>, never [demonstratively...] <meta>The text cuts off here at "apo-" with the catchword "di-", likely forming "apodictice" or "demonstratively."</meta>

<meta>catchword: demonstratively</meta>

<summary>This page argues that ancient pagan philosophers like Socrates and Aristotle used allegory and metaphorical "distillation" to understand the fecundity of God and the mystery of the Trinity. It critiques contemporary scholars who only read these ancient texts literally and fail to see the deeper theological truths hidden within them.</summary>

<keywords>Ramon Llull, Aristotle, Socrates, Hermes Trismegistus, Pimander, Monad, divine fecundity, allegory, Pagan Philosophers, sublimation, metaphysics</keywords>

# Defense of the doctrine of Ramon Llull
<page-num>292</page-num>

-> ...tasted the mystical <note>The text likely begins with "mysticum" (mystical), completing the thought from the previous page: "contenting themselves only with the [literal] sense [and never] tasted the mystical."</note> [sense]. <-

Whence so many and such great disputes arise in the sciences, without the secret hidden in phrases, comparisons, and ambiguous and enigmatic ways of speaking ever being touched upon; nor are the allegories prepared regarding that point—which has been investigated by so many men who were destroyed in its pursuit—ever fully explained. **Aristotle** tried to signify this to **Alexander the Great**, excusing himself to him concerning the publication of his philosophy; assuring him that his books were published as if they had not been published at all <note>original Latin: *suos libros editos, quasi in lucem non editos fuisse*. This refers to the tradition that Aristotle wrote esoteric works intended only for his students.</note>.

Therefore, there is something hidden in them that escapes those who read and interpret them in a common manner. I believe this also happens to the **Doctor Respondent** <note>original: *D. Resp.*, likely an abbreviation for *Dominus Respondens*, the opposing scholar in a formal academic debate.</note>. And therefore I dare to assert to him that he does not perfectly penetrate the **Logic**—for the most part—nor the **Physics** of that **Prince of Philosophers** [Aristotle] (as I would force him to admit, if he were willing to meet in a debate), which sciences he derived from the most refined of his <term>sublimates: in this context, a term borrowing from alchemy to describe highly distilled or purified intellectual principles</term>; which sciences the **Doctor Respondent** rejects only because **Ramon Llull** (as I believe) treated them through experiment. For since he was illuminated by **God** in all...

# Defense of the doctrine of Ramon Llull
<page-num>293</page-num>

...concerning the mystery of the **Most Holy Trinity**. [They focus on the] positive, without touching anything of the apodictic <note>In this context, "positive" likely refers to the study of established dogmas or historical facts, while "apodictic" refers to knowledge that is demonstrably certain through logical or mystical proof.</note>: from this, so many disputes arise in the sciences, without touching upon anything of the arcane secret that is enclosed in their phrases, comparisons, and ambiguous ways of speaking, allegorizing everything regarding the point that so many seek, and yet lose themselves in their search. This was what **Aristotle** suggested to **Alexander the Great**, excusing himself to him for having published his **Philosophy**, assuring him that his books had been published, but it was as if they had not been published at all <note>original: "que era como si no se huvieffen divulgado." This refers to a famous (likely apocryphal) letter where Aristotle tells Alexander that his "acroamatic" works are only understandable to those who have heard his oral teachings.</note>: therefore, there is something hidden in them that is concealed from those who read and interpret them as they do in the Schools <note>The "Schools" refers to the Scholastic academic tradition of the universities, which the author views as too literal-minded.</note>.

I believe the **Respondent** <note>The *Respondiente* is the academic opponent against whom the author is defending Llull's philosophy.</note> does the same: and thus I can assure him that he does not understand the greater part of the **Logic** and **Physics** of this **Prince of Philosophers** [Aristotle] (as I will make him confess, if he wishes to come to a debate) who derived it all from the most refined processes of the <term>Alambics: distillation vessels used in alchemy and early chemistry to purify substances; here the author implies that Aristotle’s insights were "distilled" or purified truths</term>, which that Gentleman vituperates <note>vituperates: speaks of or criticizes harshly.</note> so much, solely because **Ramon Llull** used them (as I imagine); for, as one enlightened by God, he achieved success in all the scien[ces]— <note>The catchword "cias" at the bottom of the original page indicates the next word is "ciencias" (sciences).</note>

<meta>This page continues the author's defense of Ramon Llull against a "Respondent" who dismisses Lull's mystical and alchemical methods. It transitions from a defense of Lull's reputation to a core theological argument about the nature of God's attributes.</meta>

# Defense of the doctrine of Ramon Llull
<page-num>294</page-num>

...in all sciences he shone with that excellence which so many historians of all Europe report and proclaim. Nevertheless, if the Respondent <note>The academic opponent mentioned on the previous page</note>, suspecting some injury in his own brain, opposes sublimations <note>original: *sublimatoriis*; likely referring to alchemical processes of purification, here perhaps used metaphorically for refined intellectual arguments or the "refined" state of Lull's mind</note>; let him permit those to deal with them and among them who, being safely endowed with mature judgment, thrive in the contemplation of their effects through the subtlety of nature being drawn out.

But setting aside this discourse, and calling upon all Hermeticists <note>Followers of the Hermetic tradition, which combines alchemy, astrology, and theurgy to understand the divine</note> to his aid and to the repulsion of this injury, I conclude my first intent. Namely, that we are bound to admit everything better and more perfect in *God*. And since fecundity <note>original: *fœcunditas*; fruitfulness or the inherent power to produce</note> and generation are of such a nature, it must be that they coexist in God from all eternity, so that all his reasons <note>original: *rationes*; in Lullian philosophy, these are the "Dignities" or the fundamental attributes of God</note>—goodness, greatness, eternity, wisdom, will, virtue, truth, glory, difference, concordance, beginning, middle, end, and equality (which are God himself, his very essence and divine nature)—may emerge equally perfect and fecund, without any sense of greater or lesser, and without contradiction.

<summary>The author defends Ramon Llull's intellectual and alchemical excellence against critics, arguing that God's divine attributes (Dignities) must be eternally fecund and equal.</summary>
<keywords>Ramon Llull, Hermeticism, alchemy, Divine Dignities, theology, fecundity, attributes of God</keywords>

### Concerning the mystery of the Most Holy Trinity
<page-num>295</page-num>

...accounts <note>original: *cias*; likely the conclusion of the word *noticias*, meaning reports or records</note> which so many historians of the vast provinces of Europe relate. And if he <note>Referring to the "Respondent" or critic mentioned on the previous page</note> has no inclination toward alambics <term>alambics: pear-shaped flasks used in distillation; here, a metaphor for the alchemical process of refining thought</term>, fearing some disruption in his own brain, let him allow those who, secured by a settled judgment, can contemplate the subtleties of nature in light of their effects to deal with them and by means of them.

But leaving this discourse aside and calling to his aid all the Hermeticists <note>Followers of Hermes Trismegistus, whose philosophy blended alchemy, theology, and nature</note> in defense against this grievance, I conclude my first point: that we must grant in God everything that is best and most perfect. And since fecundity <note>the ability to produce offspring or fruit</note> and generation are such perfections, it is necessary that they exist in God from all eternity; so that all His reasons <note>original: *razones*; in Llull’s philosophy, these are the "Dignities," the fundamental building blocks of reality that exist within God</note>—goodness, greatness, eternity, wisdom, will, virtue, truth, glory, difference, concordance, beginning, middle, end, and equality—which are God Himself, His very essence and divine nature, may be equally perfect and fecund without contradiction, superiority, or inferiority.

### Defense of the doctrine of Ramon Llull
<page-num>296</page-num>

Now, Your Lordship, Master Lawrence, you clearly recognize that we have provided a solution to the subtle arguments of the Master Respondent <note>The "Respondent" refers to a specific critic or academic opponent mentioned earlier in the text</note> (even though it seemed impossible to him); yet I did not respond with a running pen <note>original: *currenti calamo*; a Latin idiom meaning to write hastily or without due care</note>, but thoughtfully and with premeditation. For in matters pertaining to God, one must proceed calmly, gradually, and tranquilly.

From this I finally infer that the Respondent, although a great philosopher, theologian, and metaphysician, has not penetrated even a single leaf of the Lullian Tree <term>Lullian Tree: referring to the *Arbor Scientiae* (Tree of Science), a massive encyclopedia by Ramon Llull that uses a tree metaphor to organize all human knowledge into a single, unified system</term>. For if, on the contrary, he had been skilled in these matters, he would not have proceeded with such levity <note>original: *levitate*; implying a lack of intellectual depth or seriousness</note>. Pondering its reasons and arguments, he would have declared himself defeated in his errors, and renouncing and utterly rejecting them, he would most affectionately embrace Lullian truths. Then he would proclaim this "Tree of Sciences" to be like the one that stood in the middle of Paradise <note>A reference to the Tree of Life or the Tree of Knowledge in the Garden of Eden</note>. Indeed, it instructs us in the good, so that we may pursue it; and it reveals evil to us, so that we may avoid its deadly contagion. Happy are you, my Lord, if you know how to enjoy this opportunity,

Now Your *Lordship* <note>original: *vuestra M.*, an abbreviation for *vuestra Merced*, a formal title of respect translated here as Your Lordship</note>, Master Lawrence, you see that we have provided a solution to all the subtle arguments of that gentleman, even though it seemed impossible to him. By which I infer that, although the Respondent is a great philosopher, theologian, and metaphysician, he does not understand even a single leaf of the Tree of Ramon Llull <term>Tree of Ramon Llull: referring to the *Arbor Scientiae* (Tree of Science), a 13th-century work that used a tree metaphor to organize all human knowledge into a unified, logical system</term>. <meta>Following the formal conclusion and submission of Alonzo de Zepeda’s 1663 defense to the Catholic Church, this page provides a standard list of "Errata" (errors). These corrections were common in early modern printing to fix typographical mistakes or grammatical slips in both the Spanish and Latin sections of the manuscript, ensuring the theological arguments remained precise and beyond reproach.</meta>

# ERRATA <note>A formal list of corrections for printer's errors or transcription slips found in the text.</note>

->*In the Spanish text.*<-

Page 163, line 16. *them*, read *it the* <note>original: "ellas, lee el las"</note>. Page 219, line 23. *justiciro*, read *righteous* <note>original: "justiciero"</note>. Page 211, line 5. *efficient*, read *of efficient* <note>original: "de eficiente"</note>. Page 269, line 21. *podira*, read *could* <note>original: "podria"</note>.

->*In the Latin text.*<-

Page 3, line 1. *which*, read *which* <note>original: "quæ lege quas"; a correction of the relative pronoun from a neuter/feminine singular to a feminine plural form</note>. And line 5. *to those things*, read *to those* <note>original: "ad ea lege ad eas"; changing a neuter plural to a feminine plural</note>. Page 96, line 24. *whom*, read *by which we* <note>original: "quos lege quibus nos"</note>. Page 168, last line. *to units*, read *nor to units* <note>original: "ad unitatum lege nec ad unitatum"</note>. Page 182, line 11. *they are*, read *it is* <note>original: "sunt lege est"; a correction from plural to singular verb agreement</note>. Page 204, line 11. *by another*, read *others* <note>original: "alio lege alii"</note>. Page 226, line 4. *virtually*, read *virtual* <note>original: "virtualiter lege virtualem"; changing an adverb to an adjective</note>.

<summary>This page contains a list of typographical and grammatical corrections for the Spanish and Latin portions of the preceding theological defense. Such errata lists were essential in the 17th century to ensure the absolute accuracy of a work before its final distribution.</summary>
